A group inside a layer is flattened first and the layer's laid under it, so the group's are the nearer. A flat design therefore carries the scoping as data, and a mark's, a label's or an axis's own `defaults` read as "the layers and groups this came from"; a hand-written design may set them too.

**Resolution.** An element's defaults are its containers' in order, farthest first: the theme's own `defaults`, the design's, the frame the element is drawn in, and the element's own. For each container its **part defaults** are laid, then its **entry for the element's kind** over them, each by the merge rule (§3.5) over what the containers before it laid; the site's stack (§3.6) is then laid over the result. So within one container a kind entry wins over the part defaults, a nearer container wins over a farther one, and every style wins over every default:

- A mark of type `t` takes the `t` entry. A `:text` mark, a mark's inline label (§5.5) and a frame label (§6) take the part defaults alone, the labels through the mark's or the label's own containers. An axis — its line, ticks, tick labels and grid — and a legend take the part defaults alone.
- The mark-kind rule of §5.6 still holds: a part a kind does not draw is ignored, whether a default or a style gave it. A design default `fill` is drawn by a bar and ignored by a line.
- A paint given only partly (`stroke: %{width: 2}`) leaves the paint's color to the layer below, so a mark with no stroke color from any layer keeps its series color (§5.6). A default `stroke: color(:gray)` is the color of every mark of a stroked kind that names none, its series color included: a default is the color, the series color only the theme's last word.
- `:none` is no paint whether a style or a default says it, and a style's paint over it draws again.
- A furniture style — `:axis`, `:label`, `:title`, … (§3.3) — is a named style, layer 4, and wins over a default: the built-in `:label` names `font: %{family: :font_family, size: :label_size}`, so a default `font` family reaches a frame label only where its style does not name one. A design that wants every text in one face sets the theme's `font_family` or restyles the furniture.

**The theme's root defaults are every part, defined.** `Visualize.Theme.defaults/1` answers the root of a theme: the library's **base** — what an element that carries no value for a key already draws — under the theme's own `defaults` field:

| Part | Base |
|---|---|
| `fill` | `%{opacity: 1}` |
| `stroke` | `%{opacity: 1, width: 1, join: :miter, cap: :butt, placement: :center}` |
| `font` | `%{family: :font_family, size: :font_size, weight: :normal, style: :normal, line_height: 1.2}` |
| `text` | `%{offset: [0, 0], angle: 0}` |
| `effect` | `%{type: :none, radius: 3}` |
| `marker` | `%{shape: :circle, size: 64}` |
| `curve` | `%{type: :linear}` |
| `opacity`, `blend` | `1`, `:normal` |

So `stroke: color(:blue)` is a blue stroke 1 wide with butt caps unless a container says otherwise, and `explain/2` (§17.3) shows that root under every part. **The base is never written onto an element.** For a fill, a stroke, an effect, a marker and a curve it is what the backends and the mark generators already draw when the key is absent — SVG's and the canvas's initial values, and the generators' `:circle`, `64` and `:linear` — so writing it would change every chart's bytes and no chart's drawing. The font is the theme's family and size: what the built-in themes' canvas draws by default (`10px sans-serif`), and what every furniture text names through its style (§3.3); an SVG text whose style names no font takes the page's, as it always has. A paint's color and a text's anchor and baseline have no base: the mark's series color (§5.6) and each placement's own (§6.3) decide them. A theme's own `defaults` are laid as layer 1, under the design's: a house theme that draws every stroke 1.5 wide says so once.

**Nothing changes for a design without defaults.** No container's `defaults` and no theme `defaults` lay nothing, so every design drawn before this section draws byte for byte as it did.

**Validation.** A defaults node is checked by its schema: each part as the style's key of that name is (§3.1, §3.2) — a color name against the theme's table, a slot against its slots — and a key that is neither a part nor a mark type is `:unknown_key` at its path. A `{:field, f}` at a key is accepted as in a style (§3.2): it binds per datum where the element has one.

### 3.9 Sizes in units, and strokes that do not scale

**Implemented** (#651, work item #671, D-174). A size is written in any common unit — `stroke: %{width: pt(1.5)}`, `font: %{size: em(1.2)}`, `text: %{offset: [mm(2), 0]}` — and a bare number keeps its meaning: user units. A unit value is `{n, unit}`, built by `Visualize.Units` (`import Visualize.Units`, [08-utilities](08-utilities.md) §13) or written directly, and every backend gets a number: the conversion happens when the style is resolved, so SVG and a canvas draw the same width.

| Where | Accepts | Absolute (`px`, `pt`, `pc`, `in`, `cm`, `mm`) | `em` | `pct` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `stroke.width`, `effect.radius` | `:size` | converted (spec/08 §13.1) | × the element's font size | `{:no_box, key}` |
| an `effect.offset` member | `:length` | converted | × the element's font size | `{:no_box, :offset}` |
| a hatch's `spacing`, `width` | `:size` | converted | × the theme's `font_size` | `{:no_box, key}` |
| a `stroke.dash` member | `:length` | converted | × the element's font size | `{:no_box, :dash}` |
| `font.size` | `:size` | converted | × the **inherited** font size | % of the inherited font size |
| a `text.offset` member | `:length` | converted | × the element's font size | % of the labeled box: the element's width for `x`, its height for `y` |
| `marker.size` | `:size` | the side of the square of the area: `pt(6)` is an area of 8² | the side, × the element's font size | the side, % of the mark's band |
| any opacity | `:size` | `{:no_unit, :opacity}` | `{:no_unit, :opacity}` | `{:no_unit, :opacity}` |

**`em` is resolved at compile time** (#651's acceptance, modification 2), never written as a CSS `em`, so a canvas, which has no cascade, and SVG agree. The **inherited font size** is the one the defaults an element inherits give (§3.8) — each container's relative size measured against the one before it, so a design at `em(1.2)` and a frame at `em(1.5)` are 1.8 times the root — and the theme's root, its `font_size`, when no container sets one. An `em` at `font.size` is measured against that inherited size; an `em` anywhere else against the element's own resolved font size, which is the inherited one unless its style sets another. That is CSS's rule for `em`, made once on the server.

**`pct` only where a size has a box** (#651's acceptance, modification 1). A text's offset is a percentage of the box it labels — the element's bounds for a mark's inline label (§5.5), the plot area for a frame label (§6) and for a label on an element with no extent; a marker's size of the band of the mark's `x` scale, else its `y` scale, and `ArgumentError` naming the mark when neither is a `:band`; a font size of the inherited font size. A stroke's width, a dash and an effect's radius have no box, so a `pct` there is `{:no_box, key}` at its path (§10.1), naming the key. A unit at an opacity, which is a fraction, is `{:no_unit, key}`.

**A stroke that does not scale.** `stroke: %{non_scaling: true}` keeps a stroke its width on screen while its ancestors are scaled or zoomed — a map's borders, a hairline grid under a zoom. The default is to scale. The part projects to the element key `vector_effect: :non_scaling_stroke`, and each backend draws it (spec/09):
- **SVG** writes `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"` on every shape the style reaches. The property does not inherit, so a group's is written on its children rather than on the group.
- **A canvas** (#651's acceptance, modification 3) divides the width by the scale of the transform current when the width is set, `√|det|` of the context's matrix (`a·d − b·c`): a 2-wide stroke under `scale(2)` sets a `lineWidth` of 1 and draws 2 pixels wide. The `:json` and `:js` outputs and the binary stream each carry it (spec/09 §3.3.3, §4.3.5), and the hooks compute it with `getTransform()` when they draw.

**A marker or a text that does not scale is not part of this.** SVG has no attribute that keeps a glyph's size under its ancestors' scale — SVG 2's `vector-effect: non-scaling-size` is drawn by no browser — and nothing in the library zooms a chart's canvas yet, so the two backends could not agree. They come with the scenes that zoom (#620).

**Validation and storage.** The validator accepts a unit value wherever a `:size` or a `:length` goes and reports a unit not of spec/08 §13 as `{:type, :size}` or `{:type, :length}`. A unit value is stored as the tuple and written in JSON as `{"$unit": [n, "unit"]}` (§11.2). Nothing changes for a design without units: a bare number is the number it was.

### 3.10 Motion: transitions

**Implemented** (#653, work item #676, D-176). When a datum's style changes — from `:ok` to `:error`, or into the board's `:selected` — the element's new look is reached over time rather than at once, when its style says so. Motion is a property of the **look**, so it is a part of the style, laid and merged as every part is (§3.5–§3.8): a state's or a role's style can carry it, and a container's `defaults` can give it to every element below.

```elixir
[
  transition: %{duration: 200, easing: :cubic_out},
  # the fill, the stroke and the opacity
  transition: %{fill: %{duration: 400}, stroke: %{duration: 150}}
  # the fill and the stroke alone
]
```

**The part** (`:style_transition`):

| Key | Type | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `duration` | `:number` | milliseconds, `300` when absent; range `{0, 5000, 50}` |
| `easing` | `{:enum, Visualize.Ease.names()}` | the curve the change follows, a curve of `Visualize.Ease` by name — the ones a scale's and a mark's `transition` take (§4.3, #360) — `:cubic_in_out` when absent |
| `fill` | `{:node, :part_transition}` | the fill's own `duration` and `easing` |
| `stroke` | `{:node, :part_transition}` | the stroke's own |
| `opacity` | `{:node, :part_transition}` | the whole element's opacity's own |

A `:part_transition` is `duration` and `easing`, typed as above. **Which properties move:** the part's own `duration` or `easing` moves all three — `fill`, `stroke` and `opacity` — and a part entry moves that part, so `%{fill: %{duration: 400}}` moves the fill alone; each property's duration and easing are its entry's, else the part's own, else the defaults above. A `duration` of `0` moves nothing, and a node with none of the keys moves nothing. **What a property moves:** the `fill` the fill's color and its opacity; the `stroke` its color, its width and its opacity; `opacity` the element's. A gradient or a hatch is not interpolated: it is drawn at its new value at once, on every backend, as a color that changes to a paint is. Geometry is not a style's to move: a mark's own `transition` eases its readings (§5.1, §12.5).

**Resolution.** `Visualize.Chart.Style.resolve/3` writes the part on the element as `transition`, resolved: the list of `{property, duration, easing}` it moves, in the order `fill`, `stroke`, `opacity`, e.g. `[{:fill, 400, :cubic_in_out}]`; an empty list is no key. It is laid as every key is, so an element whose data chose a style with a transition (§3.6) carries it on the element, and a mark whose own stack carries one carries it on its group.

**Identity.** A transition is keyed by **which thing** changed, not by which element happened to draw it. Every element of a mark that **moves** writes `data-vis-id`: a mark moves when its own stack carries a motion part, or when its stack names a style by data (§3.6) and any style the frame knows — the design's `styles`, the theme's palette — carries one. That is a fact of the design, not of the rows drawn now, so a row's element names its identity before the row first chooses a moving style, and a canvas knows what it showed when it does. The attribute is `<frame>.<mark>.<identity>`, the frame's name, the mark's one-based position among the design's marks, and the row's identity in the board's form (spec/15 §13.2) — the identity the slot's schema declares (D-167), else the inference a pick uses — written `field=value`, several fields joined by `&` in name order; a row with neither is its ordinal among the mark's elements, `#n`. A path-per-series mark reads it from the series' first datum. A mark whose style has no motion part writes no `data-vis-id`, so every chart drawn before this section draws byte for byte as it did.

**`:css` output** (SVG). The frame writes each distinct resolved transition as one class, `vis-transition-<h>` (`<h>` the base-36 `:erlang.phash2/1` of the resolved list), added to the `class` of every element that carries it, and one `<style>` element first in the frame's group holding its rule, `.vis-transition-<h>, :where(.vis-transition-<h>) * { transition: … }`, so the children of a group whose mark carries it move as the group does, and a child's own class — of equal specificity to the first selector, of more than the second's `:where()`, which counts nothing — wins over its group's. Each property is written as the CSS properties it moves — `fill` as `fill` and `fill-opacity`, `stroke` as `stroke`, `stroke-width` and `stroke-opacity`, `opacity` as `opacity` — never `all`, which would move the geometry a mark's own transition owns. The easing is **the same curve**: `linear`, or CSS `linear()` of 21 points sampled from the `Visualize.Ease` curve at `t = 0, 0.05, …, 1`, which every backend reads (§21.6, spec/09). **Colors move in OKLab** (#653's acceptance, modification 1): CSS interpolates two colors in OKLab when either is not a legacy sRGB color (CSS Color 4), so the fill and the stroke of an element that moves its color, and of every element under it, are written as `oklch()`: a literal as `oklch(L C H)`, `/ alpha` below `1`, and anything else — a theme's `var(--vis-…, literal)`, a transform's `color-mix(…)` — as the relative `oklch(from <value> l c h / alpha)`, which a stylesheet still overrides. Only an element that moves its color is written so: every other element's colors are the strings they were. The style ends with the reduced-motion rule (below). In `:literal` mode the frame writes no class and no `<style>`: the SVG backend never writes `transition` as an attribute, so a raster draws the end state. **A compiled chart's SVG layers** (§12.4) — its static layer, each tick's SVG layer, `Visualize.Chart.Compiled.svg/2` and `step/3`, which a board's cell draws with (spec/15 §8.3) — are written the same way, each layer with the `<style>` its own classes need (#678).

**Canvas.** A canvas has no cascade and no CSS, so the hook moves it (D-75): each element carrying a `data-vis-id` is drawn inside a **motion span** — the `motion` command of the `:json` output and the `motion` record of the binary stream (spec/09 §3.3.5, §4.3.15) — naming its identity and the resolved list, each easing as its 21 samples, empty for a row whose style moves nothing, so the hook knows what that row shows before a later stream moves it. `CanvasChart` and `CanvasBinaryChart` keep, per identity, the value each property was last shown at; when a new stream draws an identity with a different target, the hook moves from what it shows to the target over the duration in its frame loop (`requestAnimationFrame`), redrawing the stream each frame with the moving values in place of the targets, colors in OKLab (spec/10 §4.5). A stream with no span costs nothing: the hook draws it once and requests no frame. The `:js` script draws the end state: it runs with no library beside it. The incremental canvas (spec/09 §5) draws the end state too: its strips are drawn once.

**Reduced motion.** A reader who asks for less motion (`prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`) gets every change at once: the frame's `<style>` ends with `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { [class*="vis-transition-"], [class*="vis-transition-"] * { transition: none !important } }`, which an inline style or a class of the design's cannot beat, and the hooks read the media query at each draw and move nothing while it matches. A design cannot turn this off.

**Validation.** The part is checked by its schema: an unknown key is `:unknown_key`, an easing that is not a curve's name `{:enum, names}`, a negative `duration` `:negative` at its path (`Visualize.Chart.Validator.Motion`).

**Amended by #677** (work item 2 of #653): every element of a moving mark is drawn on a canvas inside a `save`/`restore` its span makes, and the span's box is the element's as drawn, its own transform applied (§3.11, spec/09 §3.3.5).

### 3.11 Motion: animations

**Implemented** (#653, work item #677, D-176 amended). A style may carry a motion the element runs **on its own** while the style holds — a state that pulses, an error that blinks — rather than one a change sets off (§3.10). It is a part like `transition`, laid, merged and defaulted as every part is, so a state's or a role's style carries it and the element runs it exactly while its data choose that style:

```elixir
[
  animation: %{name: :pulse, duration: 1200, iterations: :infinite},
  # a style that pulses while it holds
  animation: %{name: :blink, duration: 800, iterations: 3, delay: 200},
  # three blinks, after a fifth of a second
  animation: %{name: "wiggle", duration: 600}
  # keyframes the host registers
]
```

**The part** (`:style_animation`):

| Key | Type | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `name` | `{:one_of, [{:enum, [:pulse, :blink, :shake, :spin]}, :string]}` | a built-in (below), or the name of keyframes the host registers; without one the part runs nothing |
| `duration` | `:number` | milliseconds of one iteration, `1000` when absent; range `{0, 10000, 100}` |
| `easing` | `{:enum, Visualize.Ease.names()}` | the curve each segment between two keyframes follows, as CSS eases between keyframes; `:linear` when absent |
| `iterations` | `{:one_of, [:number, {:enum, [:infinite]}]}` | how many times it runs, `:infinite` when absent |
| `delay` | `:number` | milliseconds before the first iteration, `0` when absent |

**The built-ins** are keyframes the library defines once (`Visualize.IR.Motion.keyframes/1`), each a list of offsets from `0` to `1` and the values there of `opacity`, `x` and `y` (a translation in user units), `rotate` (degrees, about the center of the element's box as drawn) and `scale`:

| Name | Keyframes |
|---|---|
| `:pulse` | `opacity` 1 at 0, 0.35 at 0.5, 1 at 1 |
| `:blink` | `opacity` 1 at 0 and 0.4999, 0 at 0.5 and 1: a step, written as two keyframes a hair apart so CSS and a canvas step alike |
| `:shake` | `x` 0, −3, 3, −3, 3, 0 at 0, 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.8, 1 |
| `:spin` | `rotate` 0 at 0, 360 at 1 |

**Resolution.** `Visualize.Chart.Style.resolve/3` writes the part on the element as `animation`, a map of all five keys with the defaults above in place; a part without `name`, or of `iterations` `0`, is no key. A mark that carries one, or whose data may choose a style that does, **moves** (§3.10): its elements carry `data-vis-id`.

**`:css` output.** The frame writes each distinct resolved animation as one class, `vis-animation-<h>`, and its rule in the frame's `<style>` (§3.10): `.vis-animation-<h> { animation: <keyframes> <duration>ms <easing> <delay>ms <iterations>; transform-box: fill-box; transform-origin: center; }`, the easing as a transition's is written and `<keyframes>` `vis-<name>` for a built-in, the host's name as it is. The style holds `@keyframes vis-<name>` for each built-in the frame draws, `opacity` written as `opacity`, `x` and `y` as the CSS `translate` property, `rotate` as `rotate` and `scale` as `scale` — the individual transform properties, which compose with an element's transform rather than replacing it. The class is on a **wrapper**: the element is drawn inside a `<g class="vis-animation-<h>">` of its own, so the box a spin turns about is the element's as drawn — its own `transform` included — and a symbol placed by a translation spins in place rather than about the plot's corner. The rule is the wrapper's alone, never its descendants'. **An animation is each element's**: a mark whose own style animates gives it to every element it draws, each running it about its own box — the mark's group carries none — and a row whose data chose an animated style runs it alone, on every backend alike.

**Host keyframes.** A `name` that is a string names keyframes the host defines: in `:css` the stylesheet the host loads holds `@keyframes <name>`, and on a canvas the page registers the same motion with the hooks, `VisualizeMotion.register(name, [[offset, {opacity, x, y, rotate, scale}], …])` (spec/10 §4.5). A name nothing registers runs nothing on a canvas. The name is written into a stylesheet, so it is held to a CSS identifier, `-?[_a-zA-Z][_a-zA-Z0-9-]*`, and anything else is `{:unsafe, :animation}` (§10.3).

**Canvas.** The motion span (spec/09 §3.3.5) carries the resolved animation and, for a built-in, its keyframes, so the hook holds no copy of them. The hook starts an identity's animation the first time it draws it with that animation, and runs it in its frame loop: the time since, less the delay, gives the iteration and the fraction through it; each property is read between the two keyframes about the fraction that name it, the segment eased by the samples of `easing`, a property no keyframe names left as it is. The span saves the context, translates by `x` and `y`, turns by `rotate` and scales by `scale` about the center of the element's box, multiplies the element's alpha by `opacity`, and restores after the element. An animation with iterations left keeps the loop running; a finished one draws the element as it is.

**Reduced motion.** A reader who asks for less motion sees no animation: the frame's `<style>` ends with `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { [class*="vis-animation-"] { animation: none !important; } }` beside the transitions' rule, and the hooks run no animation while the query matches. A design cannot turn this off (§21.6).

**Validation.** As the transition's: an unknown key, a name that is neither a built-in nor a string, an easing that is no curve; a negative `duration`, `delay` or `iterations` is `:negative`, and a string name that is not a CSS identifier `{:unsafe, :animation}`.

**In the examples** (#784, work item #786). The interaction page (`/interaction`) holds an **Animations** panel beside *Motion by identity* (`ExamplesWeb.AnimationsDemo`): a tile per animation, each row naming its style — `:pulse` (infinite), `:blink` (three times after 400 ms), `:shake` (four times, `:cubic_in_out`, after 200 ms), `:spin` (infinite, `:linear`) and `"wiggle"`, keyframes the page's own stylesheet defines — drawn by `Visualize.Components.Chart`, with the reduced-motion rule stated and a *Replay* that remounts the chart so the finite ones start again. The code beside it shows the `VisualizeMotion.register/2` call a canvas page makes for the same keyframes.

### 3.12 Motion: enter and exit

**Implemented** (#653, work item #678, D-176 amended). An element whose row **appears** may arrive, and one whose row is **gone** may leave, over time rather than at once — a deleted row that blows up before it disappears. Which row is which is its identity (§3.10, D-167): an identity a drawing has that the one before lacked enters, and one the drawing before had that this one lacks exits.

```elixir
[
  enter: %{name: :grow, duration: 300},
  # a new row grows from its center
  exit: %{name: :explode, duration: 700, easing: :cubic_out}
  # a gone row flies apart, then is gone
]
```

**The parts** (`:style_enter`, `:style_exit`):

| Key | Type | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `name` | `enter`: `{:one_of, [{:enum, [:fade, :grow]}, :string]}`; `exit`: `{:one_of, [{:enum, [:fade, :shrink, :explode]}, :string]}` | a built-in, or keyframes the host registers (§3.11); without one the part does nothing |
| `duration` | `:number` | milliseconds, `400` when absent; range `{0, 5000, 50}` |
| `easing` | `{:enum, Visualize.Ease.names()}` | `:cubic_out` when absent |

**The built-ins.** `:fade` is `opacity` 0 to 1 entering and 1 to 0 leaving; `:grow` is `scale` 0 to 1 about the center of the element's box as drawn, and `:shrink` 1 to 0 — keyframes like an animation's (`Visualize.IR.Motion.keyframes/1` of `{:enter, name}` and `{:exit, name}`). **`:explode`** is drawn by the hook (#653's acceptance, modification 2): the element's box is cut into a 4 × 4 grid of pieces, each the element clipped to its cell, and over the duration each piece flies outward from the box's center along the line through its own cell's center — a distance, at the end, of `(0.6 + 0.4·hᵢ)·(1.5·max(w, h) + 20)`, `hᵢ` the piece's fixed jitter `((7·i) mod 16) / 15` — turns by `((37·i) mod 90) − 45` degrees about its own center, and fades to nothing; the eased fraction drives all three. So an explosion is the same on every run and every backend.

**Resolution.** `Style.resolve/3` writes each part on the element as `enter` or `exit`, a map of `name`, `duration` and `easing`, the defaults in place; a part without a name is no key. A mark that carries one, or whose data may choose a style that does, moves (§3.10), and its elements carry `data-vis-id`. A mark's own `enter` and `exit`, like its own animation, are each element's: the mark's group carries none.

**Enter.** In `:css` output an entering element is a CSS animation, as the acceptance settled: the element is wrapped in `<g class="vis-enter-<h>">`, outside any animation's wrapper, and the frame's `<style>` holds `.vis-enter-<h> { animation: vis-enter-<name> <duration>ms <easing> 0ms 1; transform-box: fill-box; transform-origin: center; }` and `@keyframes vis-enter-fade` and `vis-enter-grow` as they are drawn; a host's name is written as it is. A browser runs it when the element is inserted, so every element enters on the chart's first paint and each new row's element on the patch that inserts it. A LiveView patch matches elements by position, so a row inserted among others may land in an element another identity held: `MotionHook` (below) restarts the enter of every element whose `data-vis-id` a patch changed. On a canvas an identity the hook has not drawn before enters (spec/10 §4.5), the first drawing included, as CSS does.

**Exit, through the hooks** (D-75, #653's acceptance, modification 2). A leaving element is not in the drawing, so the drawing cannot animate it: the hook that holds the chart does, outside the patched subtree.
- **SVG.** In `:css` output an element with an `exit` carries `data-vis-exit`, the JSON `[name, duration, samples, box, keyframes]` — the name, the duration, the easing's samples (§3.10), its box as drawn and a built-in's keyframes, `null` for `:explode` and a host's. `MotionHook` (spec/10 §20), which `Visualize.Components.Chart` and a board's cell put around a chart whose drawing names identities, records before each LiveView patch every element carrying `data-vis-exit`: a deep copy of it inside shallow copies of its ancestors up to the chart's `<svg>`, so its transforms and the paint it inherits come with it. After the patch, each recorded identity the chart no longer has is placed in the hook's overlay — an `<svg>` on `document.body` over the chart, of the chart's `viewBox` — played there, and removed when its duration ends.
- **Canvas.** The motion span carries the exit and the enter (spec/09 §3.3.5). The hook keeps, for each identity with an exit, how to draw it again — its span's commands or bytes, the transform and the paints the span opened with — and when a stream no longer draws the identity it draws that copy, over the stream, each frame until the exit ends.

A host's exit keyframes run on SVG as a CSS animation of the copy's wrapper, `forwards`, and on a canvas as registered keyframes (§3.11).

**Reduced motion.** A reader who asks for less motion sees no entrance and no exit: `[class*="vis-enter-"] { animation: none !important; }` under the media query, `MotionHook` places no copy, and the canvas hooks play neither. A gone row is gone at once.

**Through a board.** A board's cell redraws its chart when its rows change (spec/15 §8.3), and the redraw is a LiveView patch of the cell's component: the cell's `MotionHook` plays each exit, and the restarted or inserted elements their enters. **The examples** show it on the *Client-side Interaction* page: a bar per service, its state fading between colors (§3.10) and pulsing in error (§3.11), and a click on a bar deleting its row, which explodes.

**Validation.** As §3.11's: an unknown key, a name that is neither its part's built-in nor a string, an easing that is no curve; a negative `duration` `:negative`; a string name that is not a CSS identifier `{:unsafe, :animation}`.

### 3.13 Generators: styles and colors resolved at render

**Implemented** (#654, work item #680, D-177). A design holds what is known when it is built. A style that depends on the row, the viewer or anything learned later — in a multi-tenant chat, each message drawn in its *sender's* tenant theme — is a **call** the design stores and the frame resolves at render:

```elixir
import Visualize.Generators, only: [generate: 2]

[
  # stored as {:generate, :tenant_style, [{:field, :tenant}]}
  style: [:message, generate(:tenant_style, [{:field, :tenant}])],
  # a paint's color
  fill: generate(:brand_color, [{:field, :tenant}])
]
```

**Defined in a host module**, as ordinary Elixir a host tests with ExUnit:

<!-- not run: a host's module, calling the host's own MyApp.Tenants -->
```elixir
defmodule MyApp.Generators do
  use Visualize.Generators

  defgenerator tenant_style(tenant_id, ctx) do
    MyApp.Tenants.theme(tenant_id, ctx.assigns.viewer).message_style
  end

  defgenerator brand_color(tenant_id, _ctx) do
    Visualize.Color.color(MyApp.Tenants.brand(tenant_id))
  end
end
```

- **`use Visualize.Generators`** imports `defgenerator/2` and compiles `__generators__/0`, the `{name, arity}` of every generator the module defines, in definition order. `defgenerator` expands to a plain public `def`, so a generator is called and tested as any function is. Its last parameter is always the context.
- **Registration is by module** (#654's acceptance, modification 2): `config :visualize, generators: [MyApp.Generators]` for the whole host, and `generators: [MyApp.Generators]` on the options that read and draw a chart — `Visualize.Chart.from_map/2`, `from_json/2`, `apply/2`, `compile/2`, and a board's `realize/4`, `components/3` and `Visualize.Board.Server` (spec/15 §6, §8). The registry is the options' modules then the configuration's; a name two modules define at one arity is an `ArgumentError`, as is a module without `__generators__/0`. Host-compiled code is trusted: a design names a generator, never code.
- **Arguments** are a list. Each is a literal term — an atom, a number, a string, a list or a map of them — or a field binding `{:field, f}`, read from the datum by §3.2's binding rule (an element's datum, a series' first), `nil` where there is no datum.
- **The context** is the last argument: `%{theme: theme, vars: vars, assigns: assigns}` — the `%Visualize.Theme{}` the frame resolved, the values of the design's variables at application (each declared variable's given value, else its default), and the `assigns:` the host passed to `apply/2` (default `%{}`): the viewer's, in a LiveView. A board passes its own `assigns:` to every cell.
- **Typed results.** A generator in a style position — a stack entry (§3.6) — returns a **style**: a name the frame knows (the design's `styles` or the theme's palette, §3.3), a style node, or a list of them, checked as a `styles` entry is (§2.6) against the frame's theme, its color names included. A generator in a `:color` position (§3.2) — a solid paint's `color`, or a paint written short, `fill: generate(…)`, and an effect's `color` — returns a **typed color** (D-173, spec/08 §1.9), or `:none`, no paint; its names are checked against the theme's color table. A result that is anything else is a **fault at that site and applies nothing**: the layers under it show through, and the fault is logged at `:warning` as `<path>: generator <name> returned no style` (or `no color`) with the result's own fault, the path the site's — `marks[0].style`, `frames.main.axes[1].style`. A wrong-typed result never crashes the render. A style result may not hold a generator itself, nor a gradient written in place (the frame holds its gradients before it draws, §3.2); either is such a fault.
- **A generator that raises is a bug and surfaces as one** (#654's acceptance, modification 1). Nothing rescues it: the render raises with the generator's own exception, as any host function's. "Never crashes the render" covers what a generator returns, not what it raises.
- **Caching.** A generator is called once per distinct `(name, arguments)` within a site's drawing — a mark's rows, one furniture element — and the context is fixed within a render, so a generator must be pure in its arguments and context. A mark of a thousand messages from two tenants calls `tenant_style` twice.
- **Precedence.** A generator entry is layer 6 of §3.7: an author's stack lays it where it is written, above a field entry before it; a board's state entry goes after it and a trailing local edit over it. A color generator in a part takes the layer of the style that holds it.
- **Where it resolves.** At every site the frame draws — a mark and its inline label per datum, a frame label, an axis and a legend once, with no datum. Where the library reads a style without drawing it — the color domain's inference, the type inference, the builder's stack panel — a generator lays nothing, as a field entry does there.
- **Validation** (§10.1). When the chart is built, each `{:generate, name, args}` is checked against the registry the read was given (#654's acceptance, modification 2): a name no registered module defines is `{:undeclared, :generator, name}`, and a name that is registered but not for this many arguments is `{:arity, :generator, name, n}`, `n` the arguments given; `name` must be an atom and `args` a list (`{:type, :generator}`), each argument a term or a field binding checked as any field binding is. A fragment validated alone (§19.8) does not check names, as it does not check its references. A generator is accepted at a site's stack and in a solid paint's or an effect's `color` within a style a site reaches; anywhere else — a gradient's stops or a gradient paint's `color` (`{:invalid_for, type}`), `defs`, a theme, a `defaults` node, a scale's range — it is the position's type fault, since nothing there is laid by a frame that calls it.
- **JSON** (§11.2): `{"$generate": [name, [arg, …]]}`, the arguments as terms.
- **Explained** (§17.3): `explain/2` attributes each generator's result at its layer.
- **Not here:** generators written as stored source text, which need #643's checked compile path (an AST allowlist and a bounded process).

**The examples** run the tenant chat as a board (`/tenants`): one transcript, messages from two tenants, each drawn in its sender's tenant style, and a cell of messages per tenant whose bars take the tenant's brand color — the two cells over one generator module, `Examples.Tenants.Generators`, registered in the examples' configuration.

## 4. The frame

### 4.1 Keys

**Implemented.** A frame is a coordinate system and its furniture, and **a design holds several, by name** (#383): `frames` is a map, so `frames.history` and `frames.now` are two plots of the same design over the same sources, placed by their boxes. `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` realizes **one** of them into a `%Visualize.Chart.Frame{}` (§4.7) — every scale built once, the theme resolved, over the marks and labels that say that frame — and `Visualize.Chart.Frame.generate/2` renders its furniture: grid, axes, legend and labels, the static part of a chart that a data change never touches (§12).

A design with one frame names it whatever it likes; the migration of an older design names it `main` (§9).

| Key | Type | Facet | Default | Merge |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `kind` | `{:enum, [:cartesian, :polar, :geo, :facet]}` | `:geometry` | required | `:override` |
| `margin` | `{:node, :margin}` | `:geometry` | `%{top: 20, right: 20, bottom: 30, left: 40}` | `:override` |
| `scales` | `{:map, {:one_of, [{:node, :scale}, :adopted]}}` | `:geometry` | `%{}` | `:union` |
| `axes` | `{:list, {:node, :axis}}` | `:geometry` | `[]` | `:concat` |
| `legend` | `{:node, :legend}` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `projection` | `{:node, :projection}` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `facet` | `{:node, :facet}` | `:binding` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `viewport` | `{:node, :viewport}` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `box` | `{:list, :number}` | `:geometry` | `[0, 0, 1, 1]` | `:override` |
| `cell` | `{:list, :integer}` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `span` | `{:list, :integer}` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `z` | `:integer` | `:geometry` | `0` | `:override` |
| `background` | `:color` | `:style` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `defaults` | `{:node, :defaults}` | `:style` | `nil` | `:override` |

### 4.2 Size and margin

**Implemented.** **A design records no size** (#383, #427). How big a chart is is a property of where it is *shown*, not of what it says, so the size comes in at render: `Visualize.Chart.apply/2` takes `size:` as `{width, height}` — the host's container — and `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` takes the same option, `{600, 400}` by default so a static render still works and a design never carries one. The frame node therefore has no `size` key, and a design that still has one is the validator's unknown-key fault, which is what makes an unmigrated design fail loudly instead of drawing at a stale size.

**A frame's `box` is its share of the design's** (#383): `[x, y, w, h]` as **fractions** of the box the render is given (§4.2's `size:`), so side by side is `[0, 0, 0.5, 1]` and `[0.5, 0, 0.5, 1]`, a gauge in the corner of a plot is `[0.72, 0.04, 0.24, 0.24]` over `[0, 0, 1, 1]`, and two boxes that overlap draw over one another. Fractions rather than pixels because the design records no size at all: the same design fills whatever container it is given, and the frames keep their proportions. A frame's own pixel box is `[x·W, y·H, w·W, h·H]` and its margins come out of that, so each frame has its own plot area and its own margins. `z` orders the drawing, ties broken by name, so an overlay says so rather than depending on declaration order; `background` is a color painted under that frame's contents and nothing else, `nil` by default — a frame is transparent, so an overlay hides only what it paints.

**A grid of weights says where a frame goes, in place of arithmetic** (#384). The design's `layout` is a `:layout` node — `columns` and `rows`, each `{:list, :number}` of **weights** and `[1]` by default, and `gap`, a `:number` of pixels between cells, `0` by default — and a frame writes `cell: [column, row]` in place of `box`, with `span: [columns, rows]` (`[1, 1]` by default) for one that covers more than one cell. `columns: [4, 3, 2, 1]` is four columns in those proportions, so *Context*'s four windows are cells `0..3` of one row and no fraction is computed by hand.

A weight is not a fraction: the weights are **normalized over their sum**, so `[4, 3, 2, 1]` and `[8, 6, 4, 2]` are the same layout. A weight that is not positive is a fault — a frame with no width is not a frame — as is a `cell` outside the grid, a `span` that runs past its edge, and a frame that gives **both** `box` and `cell`, which are two ways of saying one thing.

**A cell resolves to a box in one place**: `Visualize.Chart.Frame.box_of/3`, which `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` reads a frame's box through (§4.7) and which a tool that must know where a frame is drawn without realizing it — the builder's graph (§18.16) — calls too; it takes the frame node, the design's `layout` and the render's size, and answers the box as fractions. The design keeps what its author wrote — `from_map/1` and `to_map/1` are inverses and a stored design says `cell`, not the arithmetic — and everything downstream reads the realized frame's `box`, so nothing else learns about cells. The `gap` comes out of the cells' own boxes and not out of the grid's outer edges: a row of cells with a gap still spans the whole box, and each frame's plot is that much narrower. A design with no `layout` and a frame with no `cell` are what they were.

The gallery draws what the boxes are for (#429): *Load* is one load source read twice — a history line over `[0, 0, 1, 1]` and a gauge of its newest reading (`:take`, §5.4.1) over `[0.72, 0.04, 0.24, 0.24]`, an overlay with its own background — and *Context* is one source at four time scales, four `:window` steps in four frames attached left to right, so a year and the last fifteen minutes are legible in one picture. *Load age + gauge* (#498) is the overlapping inset from two charts' pieces: *Load age*'s log-age view over the whole box and *Load gauge*'s dial in a box over the age plot's upper-left corner, inside its plot area, at `z: 1` on its own background, both read at one `now` (§12.6).

**Margins are pixels and do not scale.** `:margin` has `top`, `right`, `bottom` and `left`, each `:number`, `:geometry`, default `0`; the plot area is the given size less the margins, so it is the *plot* that flexes with the container while the tick text, the axis gutters and the fonts stay the size they were designed to be read at. The same design at two sizes therefore draws byte-identical furniture text and two different plot boxes. `Visualize.Chart.Schema.default/2` gives the frame's defaults so a consumer never repeats them, and the frame struct carries the plot area as `plot` (§4.7).

The `:size` **node kind** goes with it: nothing in a design declares a pair of pixels any more. A transform step's `size` key (§5.4.2) is a `{:list, :number}` and the compiled chart's own `size` (§12.1) is the render's, neither of them a node.

### 4.3 Scales

**Implemented.** A `:scale` node is one of the fourteen kinds `Visualize.Scale` constructs ([03-scales](03-scales.md) §2) with the kind's parameters; every key is `:geometry` and `:override`.

**A scale name belongs to its frame** (#383). `scales` is a key of the frame, so `frames.history.scales.x` and `frames.now.scales.x` are two scales; a frame's own axes and legend read the scales *that frame* declares, and a mark's or a label's are its own frame's (§5.1, §6.1). A name no frame in scope declares is `{:undeclared, :scale, name}` as it always was.

**A frame may adopt another frame's scale** (#384). In place of a node of its own, a frame writes `{:frame, f, s}` under a scale name — `frames.day.scales.y: {:frame, :year, :y}` — and that name is then **the same scale** as `frames.year.scales.y`: the node is the owner's, so its kind, its `nice`, its ticks and its declared domain are said once, and its **domain is shared** — inferred over the channels of *every* frame taking part, the owner's and every adopter's, so four frames over four windows of one column read against one axis. The **range stays the adopting frame's own**, since a range is pixels and each frame has its own plot: the same domain drawn at each frame's own size. An axis or a legend on the name draws the shared domain in whichever frame it stands.

A chain resolves to the first **declared** scale — an adopter of an adopter reads the node at the end of the chain — and a cycle is `{:cycle, :scale, name}` at the path of the scale that closes it. A frame the design does not declare is `{:undeclared, :frame, f}` and a scale that frame does not declare is `{:undeclared, :scale, s}`, both at the adopting scale's own path. An adopted name is a scale of the adopting frame like any other: its marks read it by name and know nothing of where the node came from.

**The domain is resolved when the design is applied** (§7.3), in two passes: every frame is realized over its own marks, then each adoption group's domains are united — by extent for a continuous scale, by the categories in order of first appearance for a discrete one — and the frames taking part are realized again with that extent given, which is the `domains:` option `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` already takes for a window (§12.5). The realized frame carries what it was given as `domains` (§4.7), and a compiled chart carries it on (§12.1), so **a tick draws the shared domain the chart was compiled with**: a compiled chart is one frame (§12.1) and the other frames' rows are not in it to be re-read. A design whose shared domain must follow moving rows on the canvas backends fixes the domain or is compiled again, which is the rule a design whose *marks* move already follows (§12.5, #424).

| Key | Type | Default | Applies to |
|---|---|---|---|
| `kind` | `{:enum, [:linear, :log, :power, :sqrt, :symlog, :time, :ordinal, :band, :quantize, :quantile, :threshold, :sequential, :diverging, :radial]}` | required | every scale |
| `domain` | `:extent` | `:auto` | every scale |
| `range` | `{:one_of, [:range, :ramp, :color_map, :name]}` | `:auto` | every scale; a list is positions or colors (`:range`, below, #710); a name is a color scheme of `Visualize.Scale.Color.schemes/0`; `{:ramp, name}` is a ramp of the theme, on a color scale (below); a map from category to color is an `:ordinal` scale's (below, #565) |
| `nice` | `:boolean` | `false` | continuous |
| `clamp` | `:boolean` | `false` | continuous, color |
| `base` | `:number` | `10` | `:log` |
| `exponent` | `:number` | `1` | `:power` |
| `constant` | `:number` | `1` | `:symlog` |
| `padding` | `:number` | `0` | `:band` |
| `padding_inner` | `:number` | `nil` | `:band` |
| `padding_outer` | `:number` | `nil` | `:band` |
| `align` | `:number` | `0.5` | `:band` |
| `round` | `:boolean` | `false` | `:band` |
| `unknown` | `:term` | `nil` | `:ordinal`: what a value outside the domain maps to ([03-scales](03-scales.md) §8.2). On the scale named `color`, and on any scale whose `range` is a map of colors (#565), `nil` is **the theme's `:axis` slot** (#487, D-122), so a category the domain lacks draws a visible neutral rather than nothing; a color, a slot or `:none` given here wins |
| `polarity` | `{:enum, [:higher_is_better, :lower_is_better, :neutral]}` | `nil` | a color scale ranging over a ramp — `range: {:ramp, name}`, or a `:sequential` or `:diverging` scale's `:auto` range, its theme ramp: which end of the domain is better, so the ramp's `0` end — `:good` on `status` — is placed there (below, #565, D-143); `nil` reads the ramp low to high |
| `transition` | `{:node, :transition}` | `nil` | continuous: how a moving domain reaches its new extent in a compiled chart (§12.5, #360) |
| `start` | `:number` | `0` | the `angle` scale: where its range begins, degrees clockwise from 12 o'clock (#390) |
| `sweep` | `:number` | `360` | the `angle` scale: how far its range runs, degrees, negative counter-clockwise; `0` and beyond a full turn are faults (#390) |
| `zone` | `:string` | `nil` | `:time`: the display zone, an IANA name, whose local calendar the ticks follow and whose wall clock the labels show ([03-scales](03-scales.md) §7.4, #447); `nil` is UTC. A name the host's time zone database cannot show is the fault `{:zone, reason}` (§10.1) |

A `:transition` node has `duration` (`:number`, `:geometry`, `300`, milliseconds), `easing` (`{:enum, …}`, `:geometry`, `:cubic_in_out` — a curve of `Visualize.Ease` by name) and `shrink_after` (`:number`, `:geometry`, `0`, milliseconds), every key `:override`. It says nothing to `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2`, which draws the inferred domain as it always has: a transition is a property of a *run*, and lives in the thing that runs (§12.5). **The same node sits on a mark** (§5.1, #417), where it eases the mark's own values instead of a domain and `shrink_after` says nothing.

**Realization.** `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` builds one scale struct per entry through the constructors of `Visualize.Scale` — `:linear` by `Visualize.Scale.linear/0`; `:log` by `Visualize.Scale.log/1` with `base`; `:power` by `Visualize.Scale.power/0` with `exponent`; `:sqrt`; `:symlog` with `constant`; `:time` with `zone` by `Visualize.Scale.Time.zone/2` when it is given; `:radial`; `:ordinal` with `unknown`, which on the `color` scale is the theme's `:axis` slot when the node gives none (D-122); `:band` with `padding` or, when either is given, `padding_inner` and `padding_outer`, then `align` and `round`; `:quantize`, `:quantile` and `:threshold`; `:sequential` and `:diverging` by `Visualize.Scale.Color` over the range — then the domain, the range, and `Visualize.Scale.nice/1` and `Visualize.Scale.clamp/2` when the flags are true. The realized scales are cached on the frame and read through `Visualize.Chart.Frame.scales/1`, the escape hatch for annotations, brush conversion and custom marks; `Visualize.Chart.Frame.put_scale/3` replaces one after construction, which is the code-level override of §1.1 — an axis drawn for that name follows the replacement (D-60).

**Inference.** `domain: :auto`, or an extent with `:auto` at one end, is inferred at construction from every mark channel that names the scale, over the sources given to `new/2`, whole column (Tucan's first-row rule is the documented limit, D-52). **A scale whose domain is fixed reads no column** (#409): the columns are gathered for the scales that infer one and no others, so a chart whose scales are all fixed — what a streaming design's copy-shift requires of every scale but its viewport's (§12.5) — never walks its rows to build the frame. `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` takes `domains:`, a map from scale name to an extent, which stands in for what would have been inferred: the compiled chart passes the viewport scale's window through it (`[hi − span, hi]`, which the window would have set afterwards anyway), so a tick of a viewport design infers nothing at all:

- A channel names a scale by the frame's kind. In a `:cartesian` (and a `:facet`) frame `x`, `x0` and `x1` name `x`; `y`, `y0`, `y1`, `median`, `inner_lo`, `inner_hi`, `outer_lo` and `outer_hi` name `y`; `series` names `color`, and so does `value` on a `:rect`. In a `:polar` frame `angle`, `x`, `x0`, `x1` and an `:arc`'s `start` and `end` name `angle`; `y`, `y0`, `y1`, `median`, the four percentile channels, `value` on a `:rose`, `inner` and `outer` name `r`; `series` names `color`, and so does `value` on a `:rect` (#391: the cartesian channels are the polar ones, so every mark is laid out in `(angle, r)` and bent, §5.2). In a `:geo` frame `series` names `color`. The `value` of a `:symbol`, a `:circle` and an `:arc` is a size or a share (§5.2) and names nothing. A mark's `scales` node (§5.1) renames what its channels name: `scales: %{x: :count}` sends that mark's `x`, `x0` and `x1` through the frame's `count` scale instead of `x`, so a scale of any other name — a marginal histogram's counts, a second `y` — is inferred from, and drawn by, the marks that name it (D-67). A `{:field, f}` on a `:color` key of a mark's style names `color` in every kind (§3.2) — **renamed by the mark's `scales` like any channel** (#565, D-143): a mark's color scale is one scale, `color` or what its `scales` node renames `color` to, and the series' paint, a color binding of its style, the inference over both, the validator's domain check and a compiled chart's regions all read that one. An overlay drawn into a chart whose `color` scale is its series' — event markers colored by severity over a line per host — renames `color` to a scale of its own and leaves the series' colors alone. A `{:field, f}` entry of a mark's style stack (§3.6) names no scale: its value is a style name, read from the datum (#644).
- The values are the channel's column of the mark's source through `Visualize.Data.Table.get/2` — the field itself, or the `f` of a `{:field, f}` — with `nil`s dropped; a number channel contributes the number, once the mark has a row to draw it on, and a `{:scale, :min}` or `{:scale, :max}` (§5.2) contributes nothing, since it is read *from* the domain. **A constant is data to inference**: a `rule` at `y: 0` holds `0` in an inferred `y` domain, which is a baseline meant to be seen, but a `:text` mark at `x: 0` holds `0` in an inferred `x` domain as well, which a caption never means — a series whose readings scroll away from zero is then drawn over the right part of its plot, and a step bound to the plot's columns (§5.4.1's `:m4`) no longer lands on them. The validator reports the second as `{:feeds_domain, scale, n}` (§10.1, #490, D-124); a text that should stand at an edge whatever the data reads `{:scale, :min}` or `{:scale, :max}`, and one that needs no datum is a frame label (§6.3). A mark whose source is not among the bound sources contributes nothing; a mark that reads its source through transforms contributes the column of the *transformed* rows — the pipeline of §5.4 runs at construction over the bound source, so a `:bin`'s `count` or a `:stack`'s `y1` infers the domain it is drawn on (D-62). A row a `:projection` clips is among those rows, with `nil` positions (§5.4.4, D-130), so inference reads every row, drawn or not, and a domain over its other columns is the same whatever the view.
- The domain follows the kind. Continuous scales (`:linear`, `:log`, `:power`, `:sqrt`, `:symlog`, `:radial`, `:quantize`, `:threshold`, `:sequential`) take the extent `[lo, hi]` of `Visualize.Data.extent/2`; `:diverging` takes `[lo, (lo + hi) / 2, hi]`. `:time` takes the extent under the values' own ordering (`DateTime`, `Date` or `NaiveDateTime` compare by their module, never by term order), an ISO 8601 string read as `Visualize.Data.Table.temporal?/1` reads it, and a value that is not temporal raises `ArgumentError` naming the scale. `:quantile` takes every value as its samples. `:ordinal` and `:band` take the distinct values in order of first appearance.
- `[0, :auto]` fixes the lower end and infers the upper; `[:auto, hi]` the reverse.
- A collapsed extent — one distinct value — stays `[v, v]`: every continuous scale maps it to the range midpoint and its axis draws one tick (D-49). The frame never widens a domain; an author who wants a readable axis fixes an end.
- Nothing bound gives the unit domain: `[0, 1]` for a continuous scale (`[1, 10]` for `:log`, whose domain excludes zero), `[0, 0.5, 1]` diverging, one day from the Unix epoch for `:time`, `[]` for the discrete and quantile scales. A frame therefore renders without any source, over its unit domains, which is what the static split needs (§12).

**Range.** `range: :auto` follows the scale's name. `x` is `[0, width]` of the plot area and `y` is `[height, 0]`; `angle` is **the sweep from its start** — `[start, start + sweep]` in radians, clockwise from 12 o'clock ([04-shapes-and-curves](04-shapes-and-curves.md) §6.2), the full turn `[0, 2π]` by default, so a 90° gauge at the top-left is `start: -90, sweep: 90` and a speedometer `start: -120, sweep: 240` (#390; a range given as a list still wins, and `start`/`sweep` on any other scale say nothing). **A categorical `angle`** (#393) is a `:band` scale on `angle`: its categories are spaced evenly over the sweep and the range is **widened by half a step at each end** so the band *centers* — where a position channel lands — span the sweep: on a **full turn** the step is `sweep / n` and the range `[start − step/2, start + sweep − step/2]`, so the first category stands at the start, the last one step before it, and the ring closes with nothing overlapping; on a partial sweep the step is `sweep / (n − 1)` and the range `[start − step/2, start + sweep + step/2]`, the categories ends-inclusive. The angle axis on it ticks every category at its band center; `r` is `[0, min(width, height) / 2]`; `color` on a discrete scale is the theme's series as slot atoms, `[:series_1, …, :series_n]`, which the legend and the marks resolve through `Visualize.Theme.resolve/3` in the mode of their render target, so a theme swap recolors the scale's output. A `:sequential` scale, whatever its name, runs along the theme's `sequential` ramp and a `:diverging` one along its `diverging` ramp (spec/08 §7.5, D-135): a color scale's output is a color, the plot area has nothing to offer it, and the theme's ramps are the colors that promise "more" and "either side of the middle" — where until #533 it ran from the `background` to the first series color, which promised neither. **A range over a ramp** (#533): `range: {:ramp, name}` on a color scale names one of the theme's ramps. On an `:ordinal` scale it **spreads the domain end to end** — the i-th of n categories (0-based) maps to `{:ramp, name, i / (n − 1)}`, a lone category to `{:ramp, name, 0}` — so `ordinal_scale(:color, domain: ["ok", "degraded", "down"], range: {:ramp, :status})` draws `:good`, `:warning` and `:bad`, four states draw at 0, ⅓, ⅔ and 1, and the outputs resolve through the theme in the render target's mode like any slot (§3.2); on a `:threshold` scale its `k` thresholds take the `k + 1` positions the same way. On a `:sequential` or `:diverging` scale the scale interpolates the ramp itself, Lab-interpolated through `Visualize.Theme.ramp/3`, `0` at the low end of the domain and `1` at the high (the midpoint at `0.5` for a diverging one). On a `:quantize` or `:quantile` scale, whose range length is its bucket count, a ramp names no count, and `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` raises `ArgumentError` naming the scale; on a position scale it raises likewise. A range given as a list still says each color explicitly — `range: [color(:good), color(:warning), color(:bad)]` maps the domain's categories to those roles one by one — and is how a design pins a category to a meaning whatever its place in the order. **Its entries are color positions** (`:range`, §1.4, #710, D-185): each takes every form a style's color does — a name of the theme's table, a transform such as `color(:blue) |> intensity(0.5)` or `shade(color(:red), 300)`, a ramp position, a literal — resolved through the theme in the render target's mode as a style's color is, and the bare `[:good, :warning, :bad]` is canonicalized to the typed form (§9). On a `:sequential` or `:diverging` scale a list of colors is the stops the scale interpolates, each resolved through the theme to its literal first, as a ramp's anchors are, so `range: [color(:background), color(:blue)]` is drawn in the colors of the theme it is drawn in. **A range given as a map of colors** (`:color_map`, #565, D-143) pins each category by name instead of by position: `%{"info" => :info, "warning" => :warning, "critical" => :bad}`, its keys any terms and its values colors as a style takes them (§3.2) — a role, a slot, a ramp position, a literal. It is an `:ordinal` scale's range, and on any other kind `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` raises `ArgumentError` naming the scale, as a ramp on a quantize scale does; the validator reports it first as `{:color_map, kind}` at the `range`. The scale's domain is found as any ordinal domain is — given, or inferred from the marks — and the realized scale keeps the members the map names, each mapped to its color, so a member the map does not name is outside the domain and draws the scale's `unknown`, the theme's `:axis` slot unless the node gives one. It is the form a feed's meaning arrives in: a schema's `status` (spec/08 §10.1) is a map from a state to a role, so `range: fact(:events, :severity, :status)` (§7.4) colors each event by what its feed says the severity means, and every theme draws that meaning in its own tones. **`polarity` places a ramp by worth** (#565, D-143): on a color scale ranging over a ramp — `{:ramp, name}`, or the `:auto` range of a `:sequential` or `:diverging` scale — `:lower_is_better` reads the ramp as it is, `0` at the low end of the domain, `:higher_is_better` reverses it, position `t` read at `1 − t`, and `:neutral` folds it about the middle, `t` read at `|2t − 1|`, so both ends are `1` and the middle `0`. On `status`, whose `0` is `:good` and `1` is `:bad`, a `:threshold` scale with `domain: [fact(:primary, :latency, :target)]`, `range: {:ramp, :status}` and `polarity: fact(:primary, :latency, :polarity)` draws a reading on the better side of the feed's target `:good` and one beyond it `:bad`, whichever way the feed says is better; a neutral feed draws both sides `:bad`, since every distance from its target is worse. `polarity` with any other range is `{:polarity, :range}` at its path: it orders a ramp, and a list or a map already says each color. Any other name follows the first axis that names it — `:top` or `:bottom` as `x`, `:left` or `:right` as `y` — and is `[0, 1]` when no axis does. A range given as a list is used as it is — which is how **one frame held two panels** (#376): a second pair of position scales whose ranges are the lower half of the plot, `y` ranging over the upper half, the second panel's marks reading the pair through their `scales` renames (§5.1), an axis per scale each spanning its own range. **Two panels are now two frames** (#383): the gallery's *Spectrum* drew its signal above its spectrum that way and is now a `signal` frame over the upper half of the box and a `spectrum` frame over the lower, each with its own `x` and `y`, which is what the trick was standing in for — an explicit range stays what it always was, the way to say where *one* scale draws. An empty list raises `ArgumentError` naming the scale — a scale with no value to map to is an authoring error, not a scale; a scheme name is `Visualize.Scale.Color.scheme/1`'s stops, the colors of a discrete scale or the interpolator of a color scale. A scale name is any atom; the names `x`, `y`, `angle`, `r` and `color` carry the meanings above, so an axis or a legend on any other name MUST say where it draws through `side` and `position`. **An offset scale** (#132): the names `x_offset` and `y_offset` are a band within a band — a `:band` scale whose `:auto` range is `[0, bandwidth]` of the scale its family maps to (`x` or `y`, or whatever the mark's `scales` node renames it to), realized after that scale; its domain is inferred from the fields the marks' `offset` nodes read (§5.1). A range given as a list is used as it is.

**A range is never styles** (#644, D-171). A scale maps data to positions and colors; a whole style is chosen by data through a `{:field, f}` entry of a style stack, whose value names the style (§3.6). A list or a map range is checked as colors: a role, `:axis` or `:grid` there is the theme slot of that name, and a style name that is no slot — `:muted`, `:style_2`, a style the design declares — is `{:undeclared, :slot, name}` at its path, as any other non-color is.

### 4.4 Axes and tick specs

**Implemented.** An `:axis` node draws a scale on one side of the plot area; tick geometry is a `:ticks` node, and what the scale's values are a `:measure` node, both the fourth level.

| Key | Type | Facet | Default | Merge |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `scale` | `{:ref, :scale}` | `:geometry` | required | `:override` |
| `side` | `{:enum, [:top, :right, :bottom, :left]}` | `:geometry` | required | `:override` |
| `ticks` | `{:one_of, [:integer, {:list, :term}, {:node, :ticks}]}` | `:geometry` | `nil` — the count derived from the axis's length | `:override` |
| `format` | `{:one_of, [:string, {:enum, [:duration]}]}` | `:content` | `nil` — the format derived from the scale's domain | `:override` |
| `prefix` | `:string` | `:content` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `unit` | `:string` | `:content` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `measure` | `{:node, :measure}` | `:content` | `nil` | `:override` — what the values on the scale are: a UCUM unit and a quantity (#536) |
| `grid` | `:boolean` | `:geometry` | `false` | `:override` |
| `offset` | `:number` | `:geometry` | `0` | `:override` |
| `radius` | `:number` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` — an axis on `angle`: where its arc is drawn, a fraction of `R` when ≤ 1, else user units; `R` when absent (#390) |
| `at` | `:number` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` — an axis on `r`: the direction of its spoke, degrees clockwise from 12 o'clock, in place of `side` (#390) |
| `style` | `{:ref, :style}` | `:style` | `:axis` | `:override` |
| `defaults` | `{:node, :defaults}` | `:style` | `nil` — the defaults of the layers and groups it was placed from (§3.8) | `:override` |

`ticks` as an integer is the count hint, as a list the explicit values, as a `:ticks` node both with the sizes: `count` (`:integer`, `:geometry`), `values` (`{:list, :term}`, `:content`), `size_inner` and `size_outer` (`:number`, `:geometry`, `6`) and `padding` (`:number`, `:geometry`, `3`) — the setters of [05-axes-and-formatting](05-axes-and-formatting.md) §1.2 by name. Explicit values beat the count, and the count beats the derived count.

Each axis is a `Visualize.Axis` on the realized scale, oriented by `side`, with the tick spec, `offset` and the frame's theme applied, generated in the `:resolve` mode of the render (spec/05 §1.4) and placed at its side of the plot area: `:bottom` at `translate(0, height)`, `:right` at `translate(width, 0)`, `:top` and `:left` at the origin. A frame's axis is therefore byte for byte the axis a caller builds by hand from the same scale with the same count and format, and `test/visualize/chart/frame_test.exs` holds it to that.

**Derived defaults** (#532, D-134). What the design leaves out, the axis derives from what the realized frame already knows, its scale's domain and its own size, so no host computes a presentation rule and passes it in as a variable:

- **the count**, when `ticks` gives none (`nil`, or a `:ticks` node without `count`), is `Visualize.Axis.tick_count/2` of the axis's length in pixels ([05-axes-and-formatting](05-axes-and-formatting.md) §1.5): the span of its scale's range — the plot's width or height for a scale over the plot, the band a frame's own range gives otherwise — on a `:top` or `:bottom` axis at 80 px a tick, on a `:left` or `:right` one at 35; an axis on `angle` is as long as its arc (radius × sweep) at 80, an axis on `r` its spoke at 35 when the spoke runs more up and down than across, else at 80. At least 2 and at most 10, ten being the count every axis asked for before;
- **the format**, when `format` is absent, is `Visualize.Format.Auto.formatter/2` of the realized scale with that count (spec/05 §3.1): a time scale's ticks in the format of its span — clock times within a day, days within a week, dates within a season, months beyond — and a numeric scale's by its magnitude — plain below 1,000, grouped from 1,000, one SI prefix from a million; a band or ordinal scale's labels stay the default label. The locale is `"en"`.

An explicit count, list or `format` always wins, and `prefix` and `unit` wrap the derived label as they wrap a given one. Both are computed on **every realization**: an axis on a fixed scale derives the same count and format each time, so its region stays static (§12.2); an axis on a moving scale — inferred, eased or windowed — re-derives them per tick from the domain that tick draws (§12.5), and a resize, a recompilation at the new size, re-derives the count.
 `format` is a `Visualize.Format` specifier applied to every tick value: `Visualize.Format.time/2` on a `:time` scale — of the tick shifted into the scale's zone by `Visualize.Scale.Time.local/2`, so an explicit tick value prints in local time like a computed one (spec/05 §2.8, #447) — `Visualize.Format.formatter/1` on any other, a tick value that is not a number passing through `to_string/1` — or the name of a format no specifier can express: `:duration`, `Visualize.Format.duration/1` over a value in seconds, the unit chosen by magnitude (`45s`, `2m30s`, `1.5h`; spec/05 §2.9, #191). Bytes by magnitude are the specifier's own `s` type with a unit (`format: ".1~s", unit: "B"` prints `2.3GB`). **A tick carries a unit** (#135): `prefix` and `unit` are literals put before and after every tick's label — the formatted value, or the default label when there is no `format` — so `unit: "M"` prints `1412M`, `prefix: "$", unit: "T"` prints `$5T`, and `unit: "%"` prints `45%` of a value already in percent, where the specifier's own `%` would multiply by a hundred. The specifier stays d3's; a unit is what every caller was concatenating by hand, and the two keys live on the axis beside `format`, the key they complete, rather than on the `:ticks` node, which holds geometry.

**A measure** (#536, D-138). `measure` says what the values on the axis's scale *are*: a `:measure` node of `unit` (`:string`, `:content`) — a UCUM code ([05-axes-and-formatting](05-axes-and-formatting.md) §4.1: `ms`, `By`, `%`, `{request}/s`) — and `quantity` (`{:enum, Visualize.Quantity.all()}`, `:content`) — one of the fixed list of §4.2 there (`:duration`, `:information`, …) — either or both, completed by `Visualize.Quantity.resolve/1`: a unit alone implies its quantity, a quantity alone means its base unit. **They are facts about the data, not knobs**: a measure does not change a value, it says what the value already is, so the axis can choose how to show it; what to show stays the design's (`format`, `ticks`). The node is the shape of a field description's `unit` and `quantity` (#531), which is the seam #535 fills: a feed's schema will supply the measure of an axis whose scale plots one of its fields, and an axis's own `measure` will win over it.

On a numeric scale (`:linear`, `:power`, `:sqrt`, `:log`, `:symlog`, `:radial`), an axis with a measure and no `format` writes its ticks with `Visualize.Quantity.formatter/3` of the realized scale, the measure and the derived count, in place of `Visualize.Format.Auto.formatter/2` (spec/05 §4.3): the values **scaled into the display unit that fits the domain** and followed by its symbol — an axis on 0–1,500 `ms` reads `0 s`, `0.5 s`, `1 s`, `1.5 s`; on 0–900 `ms`, `0 ms` … `900 ms`; on 0–2,500,000 `By`, `0 MB` … `2.5 MB`; a `:count` in SI from a thousand (`45k`); a log axis gives each tick its own unit. Like the derived format it is re-derived on every realization, so a moving domain moves its display unit. An explicit `format` wins and the ticks are written by it, unscaled and unsuffixed; `prefix` and `unit` wrap either as they wrap any label. On any other scale the measure leaves the ticks alone.

**The ticks are round in the unit they are read in** (#594, D-156). When the display unit differs from the scale's unit, the axis takes its tick values from `Visualize.Quantity.ticks/3` of the realized scale, the measure, the derived count and the render's system (spec/05 §4.3): round numbers in the display unit, placed at their values converted back into the scale's unit, and labeled with those round numbers — an axis on 0–100 `s` reads `0 min`, `0.2 min` … `1.6 min`, and on 0–40 `kg` under `system: :us`, `0 lb` … `80 lb`. The grid draws at the same values. Explicit tick values win, and so does an explicit `format`, whose ticks stay the scale's own in the scale's unit; an explicit count only sets the count. A log or symlog axis keeps its powers of ten, each in its own unit.

**The unit system is the viewer's, not the design's** (#594, D-156). Which family a measure is shown in — `:metric`, `:us`, `:uk` or `:as_posted` (spec/05 §4.2) — is a preference of whoever is looking, as the size is a property of where a chart is shown (§4.2): a design records none, and the schema has no key for it. It comes in at render as `system:`, an option of `Visualize.Chart.apply/2` and `Visualize.Chart.compile/2` (default `:as_posted`, D-138's display scaling, so a chart rendered without it draws as before), which the realized frame carries as its `system` and a compiled chart keeps for every realization it makes per tick (§12). Every axis of the frame reads it — its display unit, its ticks, its format and whether it needs a title — and so does the chart's text alternative (§21.1), whose values are written in the axis's terms. A value that is not one of `Visualize.Quantity.systems/0` raises `ArgumentError` when the frame is realized. A board passes it through to every cell (spec/15 §6, §8.3).

**The axis says its measure once.** By the label rule of spec/05 §4.3 — **the unit, else the quantity, else nothing** — the unit is said on the ticks when they carry it. When they do not — the unit's symbol is empty (`1`, `{request}`), the axis has an explicit `format`, or its scale is not numeric — a cartesian axis draws a **title** of `Visualize.Quantity.label/1` of its resolved measure, in the display unit when it scales under the render's system: `request` for `{request}`, `count` for a bare `:count`, `ratio` for a `:ratio` in `1`, `ms` for an axis on `ms` with `format: ",d"`. The title is placed and styled as a frame label anchored at `{:axis, s}` is (§6.3) — the `:label` style, the `class="label"` text — inside the axis's group, so it is part of the axis's region (§12.2) and carries the axis's path; a design that anchors a label of its own at that axis's scale gets no title from the measure, its label being the title. An axis on `angle` or `r` in a polar frame draws none.

The validator checks a measure (§10.1): a `unit` that does not read is `{:unit, code, reason}` at `[..., :measure, :unit]`, and a unit whose dimension is not its `quantity`'s is `{:quantity, quantity, code}` at `[..., :measure, :quantity]`.

`grid: true` draws a line at each tick position across the plot area — the height for a `:top` or `:bottom` axis, the width for a `:left` or `:right` one — in the `:grid` style, behind every axis, in one group `class="grid"` that is `aria-hidden` like the axes (D-56). The axis's line, ticks and labels draw in its `style`; tick sizes stay geometry. A `style` other than `:axis` restyles the generated axis: `stroke`, `stroke_width`, `stroke_dasharray`, `stroke_linecap`, `stroke_linejoin` and `stroke_opacity` reach the domain line and the tick lines, `fill` and the font keys reach the tick labels, `opacity` and `class` the axis group.

An axis has no `id`: its identity in a stack is `{scale, side}` (§8.2), so a layer that wants the shared frame's bottom x-axis with six ticks writes an axis for that scale and side and replaces it, and two axes drawing one scale on one side can never both be drawn. Nothing in the axis node is required to make that identity — `scale` and `side` are both required keys already.

### 4.5 Legend

**Implemented.** A `:legend` node explains one scale, normally the color scale.

| Key | Type | Facet | Default | Merge |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `scale` | `{:ref, :scale}` | `:geometry` | required | `:override` |
| `position` | `{:enum, [:top_left, :top_right, :bottom_left, :bottom_right, :top, :bottom, :left, :right]}` | `:geometry` | `:top_right` | `:override` |
| `inside` | `:boolean` | `:geometry` | `true` | `:override` |
| `title` | `:text` | `:content` | `""` | `:override` |
| `prefix` | `:string` | `:content` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `unit` | `:string` | `:content` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `style` | `{:ref, :style}` | `:style` | `:label` | `:override` |
| `wrap` | `:boolean` | `:geometry` | `true` | `:override` |
| `max_label` | `:integer` | `:geometry` | `nil` | `:override` |
| `values` | `{:list, :term}` | `:content` | `nil` | `:override` |

The frame builds the legend from the realized scale: one entry per value of `Visualize.Scale.ticks/2` — the domain of a discrete scale, five ticks of a continuous one — each a 12 × 12 swatch and, 16 user units to its right, the value's label as an axis would print it (spec/05 §1.5), with the legend's own `prefix` and `unit` before and after it (#135), so a legend explaining a scale with units agrees with the axis drawing it. The swatch is filled with the scale's value for the entry when that is a color — a string, or a theme slot resolved through `Visualize.Theme.resolve/3` (§4.3) — and with the theme's `{:series, i}` for the i-th entry of a scale that yields anything else. The labels and the title take the legend's `style`.

```
group  style: %{class: "legend", font_family: <font_family>, font_size: <font_size>}, transform: translate(x, y)
├── text   style: %{fill: <fill>, font_weight: :bold}, content: title            — when the title is given
└── group  style: %{class: "legend-item"}, attrs: %{"data-series" => value}, transform: translate(…)  — one per entry
    ├── title  content: the full label                                  — when max_label cut it
    ├── rect  0 0 12 12, style: %{fill: <swatch>}
    └── text  x: 16 (or −4, anchored end), y: 6, dy: "0.32em", style: %{fill: <fill>}, content: label
```

Entries are 16 apart and the title, when given, is the first row. `position` places the legend inside the plot area, 8 user units from its edges: the four corner positions stack the entries downward from the corner, the right-hand ones laid out right to left — the swatch at the edge and the label anchored `end` to its left — so no text width is needed; `:left` and `:right` stack them likewise, centered on the plot's height; `:top` and `:bottom` lay them in a row centered on the plot's width, each entry's width taken as 16 plus 0.6 × the font size per character of its label (`Visualize.Chart.Text.width/2`) and the title's as its estimated width, each followed by 8 before the next.

**A legend of declared values** (#643, D-194). With `values` — a list, most often `fact(:hosts, :"health.state", :values)`, the field's declared values read from its schema (§7.4, spec/08 §10.1) — the legend explains a mapping by value rather than a scale, and `scale` is not required: one entry per value, in order, labeled by the value with `prefix` and `unit` around it, its swatch the fill of the **style the value names** (§3.6: the design's `styles`, then the theme's palette), else that style's stroke color, else the theme's `{:series, i}`. So a style chosen by `{:field, :"health.state"}`, or a `:choose` whose children are styled by the same names, has its legend built from the field's declared values with no list of its own. With both `scale` and `values`, `values` wins.

**A top or bottom legend wraps to the plot's width** (#630, D-169). With `wrap: true`, the default, the title and the entries flow left to right, in order, and a cell that would end past the plot's width starts a new row; rows are 16 apart, the first at the legend's origin, and each row is centered on the plot's width on its own. A legend whose cells all fit one row is that one row, exactly as it was before wrapping existed. `:top` inside the plot puts its first row 8 below the plot's top edge, and `:bottom` its last row's swatches 8 above the plot's bottom edge, so a wrapped legend grows into the plot from the edge it names. Wrapping places only the entries' groups — every entry keeps its group, its `data-series` and its swatch-then-label children — so `LegendHook` reads a wrapped legend as it reads a row. Two things cannot be fitted, and `Visualize.Chart.Frame.new/2` raises `ArgumentError` for each, naming the side (`top` or `bottom`) and the width needed beside the plot's width: with `wrap: false`, a row wider than the plot; with wrapping, a single cell — an entry, or the title — wider than the plot on its own, for which `max_label` is the remedy. The four corner positions and `:left` and `:right` stack, and `wrap` does nothing for them.

**A label can be cut** (#630, D-169). `max_label`, a count of characters at least 1, cuts every entry label longer than it — at every position, in the plot or in a margin — to `max_label − 1` characters and `…`, so the cut label is `max_label` characters.
