| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.insert/3` | a use inserted as the sibling after another (at the end for `nil`), or with `{:into, id}` appended inside that group |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.insert_at/3` | a use inserted before the row at a flat position, in that row's container; at the end past the last row |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.group/0` | a group's local: an inline composite with no objects |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.between/3` | `between(uses, anchor, id)`: the ids from the anchor to a sibling, in tree order, or `:error` when they are not siblings (§18.8, #382) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.grouped/3` | `grouped(uses, ids, group)`: sibling sites wrapped in the group at the first one's position, in their order; `:error` when they are not siblings (§18.8, #382) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.ungrouped/2` | an inline group's sites spliced in at its position, the group gone; any other site leaves the tree unchanged |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.duplicated/2` | `duplicated(uses, use)`: a copy of a site and what it holds with fresh ids among the tree, and the `{copy id, original id}` pairs, so labels follow |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.get/2` | the use with an id anywhere in the tree, or `nil` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.update/3` | one use, anywhere in the tree, replaced by a function of it |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.write/4` | a write into a use's `local` at a path: `{:ok, value}` sets, `:error` removes (§18.5) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.write_at/4` | the same write into any fragment — an opened entry's body |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.move/3` | a move by flat positions: the row at `from` (with what it holds) out, then before the row standing at `to` in that row's container; nothing to remap (§19.3, #380) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.kinds/0` | the kinds *new fragment of a kind* offers, the composite first and never a design (§19.6, §19.8) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.empty/1` | the empty, valid fragment of a kind |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.open/2` | an entry as the stack: a composite's sites labeled by the store's names, with its vars; any other body as one inline site named for the entry |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.structure/2` | the stack as a composite over its vars: what saving sends, and what a composite stores |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.body/2` | the lowest tier that holds the stack (D-99): one bare inline site is its body, anything more is structure |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.sites/3` | each site that carries variables — its fragment's free variables *before* the site's bindings, and what the site binds them to — for the per-site rows (§19.10) |

Selection is a use's id, and a host that seeds one (`selected`) is honored when the stack has it; disabling is the mask of everything, and its whole effect is in `design/3`. Saving sends the structure — `%{composite: %{uses, vars}}` — and never a flattened design; a host that wants the design asks `Visualize.Chart.flatten/2` with the store's fetch.

**Opening is a chart** (#243; this replaces the one-open-thing model of the first draft, in which the workspace was stashed while an entry was open). The workspace holds several **charts** at once, one **selected**; each chart is a stack — the compose face of one thing — and the panel of §18.8 lists them. The chart the host seeded is the first; its layers take anything. Clicking a library entry opens it *as a new chart* — a composite's sites, labeled by the store's names, or any other body as one inline site named for the entry — selected, with the entry recorded on it so that *Save as* writes the entry; the field forms write to the selected site's local, which for an opened style is the style's own body. Closing a chart removes it from the workspace and selects its neighbor; the last chart is not closed. Opening another entry adds another chart, as opening a second file does. Dragging an entry into the selected chart composes it, as a use referring to its id whose label is the entry's name, and the composer **admits only the chart's kind**: a chart opened on a style refuses a frame by name — *a style composer takes a style; Frame::wide is a frame* — while a chart that is a composite takes anything. The chart's row says which: its name, and its kind when it is not a composite.

**What an open kind shows.** A composite or a design has the node tabs, the preview and the design's faults from `apply/2`; any other kind has its own form as the one root — a wrapper body's nodes are its kind's, so `%{style: %{…}}` opens on a `style` tab — no preview, since there is no chart to look at, and the faults of per-kind validity in their place. A theme is the exception that has something to look at: it opens the theme mode of §20, whose editor replaces the form and whose preview sheet fills the main column. The document panel exports the fragment itself in the JSON form, valid as its kind.

**Save writes the lowest tier.** The workspace and a composite are saved as structure. Any other kind is saved as `body/2` gives it — one bare inline site is its body, values and nothing else, so a style stays a style; a stack of sites is structure under the kind's id — and is refused when what it flattens to is no longer that kind (*opened as a style and is now a frame*) or is not valid as one. A singular wrapper cascades as a node of its kind, so two style fragments stack key by key as two styles would (§8.2), and a stored structure of kind `style` flattens to a style wherever it is used.

A field in any form shows a **variable as a variable** — as a **chip**, `var(:color)`, in the place of its control, never as a struct's text in a textarea. A variable reaches a field one way only: its chip, dragged from the variable panel (§19.10) and **dropped on the field**, which writes `var(:name)` at that key (`bind_field`); dropped on a **strip tab**, it binds the first key of that root the variable fits (`bind_root`). The chip carries an **×** that unbinds — the key is removed, and the typed control returns empty and waiting (`unbind_field`). A field's own value can go the other way: every literal field has a handle, and dropping it on the panel's create area **promotes** the value into a new variable named for the key, with the value as its default, and binds the field (`promote`); a second promotion of the same key takes the next free name. The form therefore has no literal/variable switch and no name box — the `key.mode` and `key.var` controls an earlier draft carried are gone — and a field is a control or a chip, nothing else.

**A variable has the type of its first use** (§17.2). Dropping a chip on a field of another type is refused by name — *`unit` is a `:text`; `opacity` takes a `:size`* — and a variable used nowhere yet takes the type of the first control it lands on, by landing there. A **field is a channel** for this rule — a channel is a field or a constant — so a variable typed by a mark's channel fits a source's field list and one typed by a field list fits a channel; and a list of scalars takes a chip as an item of its element's type. One function says what a change to the form means:

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.change/2` | what a change to a form means: the key, and `{:ok, value}` or `:error` for a removal; `nil` for a key the kind lacks |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.submitted/2` | a control's value read as a type, with the checkbox rule `parse/2` cannot know |

The names on offer are those the stack declares under `vars` and those any layer already uses, listed by the variable panel.

### 19.10 Variables, and the context panel

**The variable panel** is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Variables.panel/1`, in the context panel: the composition's variables — what the stack declares under `vars` and what any layer still uses, `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Variables.list/2` — **alphabetically**, each a chip (§19.9) beside the word for its type. A chip **expands** to its details: every use as *layer → path*, each a link that opens that node (§18.9); the **default** of its declaration — written where the stack declares the name, the highest site that does (a `var` site by its key, or a layer under its `vars`), else declared by a new `var` site on top so it is what wins (`set_default`); the **value** the preview binds it to (§18.11's control, one per variable, writing `set_var`) with the default as its placeholder; and the **per-site rows** below — one `<form class="vis-builder-site">` per site whose fragment carries the variable (from `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.sites/3`), reading as one of three — *flows up as `:color`*, `= "blue"`, `= var(:color_1)` — with a value box and a variable box; whichever changed last is what the site says, a blank in either is a removal, and `bind_site` writes `Use.vars` and nothing else, which is §19.4's rename. A **find** box filters the list, and a name typed whole highlights the chip and every field bound to it. A **create** form declares a new variable as a `var` site at the bottom of the stack, keyed by its name, with its default (`create_variable`); the same area is where a field's handle is dropped to promote (§19.9). Variables are not a root of the design; the strip's last tab opens this panel (§18.13, #363), badged with how many variables still need a value.

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.variable/1` | a typed variable name as the variable it names; a blank is `:error`, a removal |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Variables.panel/1` | the variable panel: chips alphabetically, find, create, and each expanded chip's uses, value and per-site rows |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Variables.list/2` | every variable the composition declares or uses, alphabetically, typed by its first use |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Variables.type_of/1` | the type a variable took from its first use, or `nil` for none |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Variables.unbound/3` | the variables with neither a default nor a binding, alphabetically — the count the variables tab's badge shows (§18.13, #363) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.label/1` | a path as a person reads it — the spelling the validator uses for an error's path |

**The context panel is one of three** — *inspect*, *color*, *variables* — one open at a time (`context`), *inspect* holding the form and the layer's context and opened by every selection, *color* by its toggle and *variables* by its tab at the end of the node row (§18.13, #363, #366; the icon row of panels is gone, its *inspect* button having done nothing a selection did not), under the node icons and chips (§18.16), each staying put while a person works, the way a drawing tool keeps its pickers beside the canvas. Two panels of the first row of five are now under the tab they doubled (#314): the chart's signals are sources, so they stand under the **sources** root — *inspect* over it shows the sources page and, beneath, the signals panel (below) — and the font keys are a style's, so the font panel stands under the **styles** root, beneath its page, over the node being edited:

| Panel | Shows | Writes |
|---|---|---|
| **inspect** (the default) | the selected node's form (§18.7), then the selected site's key for a name-keyed kind and its free variables (§19.5, §19.3) — its mask is under its row (§18.8); at a style site, the site's stack and the chosen entry's resolved style (§18.18, §18.19) | `key`, the style stack |
| **color** | `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Picker.panel/1`, the chooser: the selected color's **chain** (below) when it is one, then **patches above the picker** — the theme's color table **by name, grouped by use** (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Picker.groups/1`: series, roles, furniture, the theme's own colors, and the CSS named colors folded under a disclosure), each patch in the color the theme gives it and titled *name · value*, then the color literals the chart already uses — then a grid of hues at three lightnesses and a picker for any color; with a color field selected they write it, with none they show only — and every patch is a color a person can **drag onto any well** (below) | the selected color field, through `edit`; its chain, through `color_chain`; any well, through `drop_color` |
| **inspect** over `styles` | beneath the page, `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Fonts.panel/1`: the families rendered in themselves, size as a slider, the face list of §18.6, text anchor — the font keys of the node being edited, together; a node with none says which to open | a style's `font.family`, `font.size`, `font.weight` and `font.style` (as `font.face`) and `text.anchor`, a theme's `font_family` and `font_size`, through `edit` |
| **inspect** over `sources` | the **data page** in place of the root's sections (#372): one row per source — its name, its columns with their types, its generator while designing, or the host's rows — with +, ×, play and pause, the tick's period (below) | `add_source`, `remove_source`, `rename_source`, `add_column`, `rename_column`, `remove_column`, `set_column_type`, `set_generator`, `set_default_source`, `edit_signal`, `play`, `pause` |
| **variables** | the variable panel, above | `Use.vars`, bindings, declarations |

**Selecting a field opens its panel.** Clicking into a field marks it (`select_field`): the form's `Visualize.Hooks.Builder` is the one listener for clicks — a click on a color well selects the well's own field, which for a gradient's stop is `fill.stops.<i>.color` (§18.6), and any other click in a row selects the row's key — and a key pressed in a row selects it too (`phx-keyup`); a row carries no click binding of its own, so one click is never two selections. A color field opens the color panel; a font key, and any other field, leaves the panel where it is — a font key is a style's, and the font panel is under the styles root the style's form is in. Selecting a node, a facet or a layer clears the field, and **selecting a layer, a node or a root opens *inspect*** — the tab a site's key lives in, so a source's list is in sight whenever its site is selected, whichever picker was open before. Every control in a picker writes the same `edit` the form does under the same names (`key`, `key.slot`, `key.range`), so a picker is a bigger view of the form's fields and never a second form; the per-field faces of an earlier draft — glyphs, words, references and sliders in the context — live in the form itself (§18.6) and have no second home.

A bare `<input type="color">` would drop the theme slot, and the theme slot is how the built-in styles work; the well shows whichever face the value has, and the chooser offers every face.

**A name picked is a typed color** (#683, D-178). A patch of the color table writes `color(:name)` — `{:color, name}` (spec/08 §1.9) — through `key.slot`, so what the builder stores is the typed form the design's canonical form holds and never a bare atom a reader must guess at. **A picked color is the chain's base**: where the field holds a transform, a name, a hue or the picker's color replaces the base the transforms apply to and keeps every step, so `color(:red) |> shade(700) |> alpha(0.8)` with *blue* picked is `color(:blue) |> shade(700) |> alpha(0.8)`; a drop on its well is the same write.

**A transform is a chain, edited step by step** (#683, D-178). A color the design writes as a pipe of `Visualize.Color`'s transforms is the term nested inside out, `{:alpha, {:shade, {:color, :red}, 700}, 0.8}`; the chooser shows it as it reads — the **base**, then each **step** in the order it is applied — `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.split/1` and its inverse `join/2`. Each row is a swatch of the color reached so far, resolved through the design's theme in `:literal` mode (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.rows/2`) — so a person sees what every step does, live, as the amounts change — then the step's word, its **amount** as a ranged box (0–1 for `intensity`, `alpha`, `lighten`, `darken`, `saturate`; 0–1000 by 50 for `shade`; `contrast` has none) and an **×** that removes it. A **+ step** select adds `intensity`, `alpha`, `lighten`, `darken`, `saturate`, `shade`, `contrast` or `blend` at the end at its starting amount (0.5, 0.5, 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, 500); `blend` makes the color the first operand of a blend with the theme's `background`. A blend's base lists its **operands**, each a chain of its own, edited the same way at its path (`1`, `1.0`), with a **+ operand** and an **×** on each while there are more than one — a blend of one is its operand, so the last × dissolves it. Every edit is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.edit/3` of the value — a function of the color to the color — written to the field as any write is (§18.7), so the chain builds exactly the term `Visualize.Color`'s helpers would and nothing else. The events are `color_chain` (`field`, `path`, and `op`: `add` with `step`, `amount` with `index` and `value`, `remove` with `index`, `operand` with `add` or `remove`), a no-op where the field is not a color.

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Picker.groups/1` | the theme's color table by name grouped by use, each name with the color the theme gives it: series, roles, furniture, the theme's own, the CSS names |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.split/1` | a color as its base and the steps applied to it, in order |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.join/2` | a base and steps as the color they mean; `split/1`'s inverse |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.edit/3` | `edit(color, path, change)`: the color with one step added, changed or removed, its base replaced, or a blend's operand added or removed, at the operand `path` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.at/2` | the color at an operand path of a chain, or `nil` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.rows/2` | the chain as the chooser draws it: each row's word, amount and span, and the color reached so far through the theme |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.steps/0` | the steps the chooser offers, in order |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.span/1` | the span a step's amount takes |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Chain.rebase/2` | `rebase(current, picked)`: a picked color as the new base of the chain a field holds, its steps kept; the picked color itself where the field holds no steps |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.named/1` | a name of the color table as a patch submits it, read as `color(:name)`; a blank is `:error`, a removal |

**The chooser's table costs one pass over the theme** (#704, D-184). `Picker.groups/1` is a function of the theme alone; the editing pass (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editing.editing/1`) builds it once and every color field reads that one. A name is tested against the theme's table with `Visualize.Theme.color_name?/2` (spec/08 §7.6), which builds no list: `Chain.resolved/2` once rebuilt, de-duplicated and sorted `Theme.color_names/1` for every name it resolved, so the groups cost the square of the table's ~190 names and the board-mode builder test ran out of its 60 s under the suite's load. The editing pass is held to a reduction budget (spec/12 §6). **The pool's column types are typed once per change of the data** (#714, D-189): the types `Visualize.Data.Table.column_types/1` reads from the host's `sources` and from each catalog feed's preview rows (§22.8) are computed when the host gives them — `update/2`, keyed by the very data they were read from — and every pass reads them; a pass given data other than what was typed types it itself, so a type is never stale. The signals (§19.10), whose rows move with every tick, are typed in the pass, and the pass types its pool once for every field, the channel columns and the data page that read it. Before #714 a board-mode pass typed the gallery's 76 sources twice and every catalog feed once, about a fifth of its work. **A board's previews and its faults are kept by their inputs** (§22.5, §22.6, #723, D-191): before #723 every pass drew every cell's preview and validated the whole board, about 1.4 million and 0.3 million reductions of the examples' 2.1 million.


**A color field is a well.** Every `:color` key in a form — a paint's `color`, a stop's, a shadow's, a theme's `axis` — renders as one square showing the color the value resolves to: a slot or a typed color through the theme the design names (`Visualize.Color.resolve/3` in `:literal`), a literal as itself, a paint as its gradient's first stop (§18.6), `:none` as a hatched square, an unset key as an empty one; the slot's or literal's word is the well's `title`, and a bound variable shows as its chip in the well's place (§19.9). There is no select of slot names and no picker in the form: a name where the only thing wanted is the color, and a picker under every color key, were clutter, and both wrote the key the chooser writes. Clicking the well selects the field and opens the chooser in the context (`select_field`, above). A well is also a **drop target** (`data-builder-well`): every patch in the chooser — a slot, a hue, a color the chart uses — is draggable (`data-builder-color`, carried as `application/x-visualize-color`), and a patch dropped on a well writes that well's key whether or not the field is selected, so one color is carried to several fields without selecting each. `drop_color` carries the field and the color: a word writes the slot as the atom, a `#rrggbb` writes the literal, both through the same `edit` the chooser uses (`key.slot`, `key`), and a key that is not a color refuses by name. A well in the style panel of the layer context (§18.19) takes a drop the same way, through `edit_style`.

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Picker.panel/1` | the chooser: patches — slots and the chart's colors — above the hues and the picker, each patch draggable, writing the selected color field through `edit` or showing with nothing to write |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.well/2` | what a color well shows for a value and a theme: the resolved color, `:none`, or nothing |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Picker.face/1` | which picker a field would get from its key and type: color, font, glyphs, enum, reference, range or plain |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Fonts.panel/1` | the font panel over the node being edited: families, size, the face list, anchor, through `edit` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.StyleForm.body/1` | a style's form in its parts: the whole element's keys, then a fieldset per part, paint type first, the parts the element kind does not draw grayed (§18.7) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.StyleForm.locate/2` | a control's name read back through the schema: the key spec at that path of a style, the path, and the control's suffix; `nil` for no key |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.StyleForm.write/4` | `write(node, path, parsed, seed)`: the top-level writes one edit at a path means — the part it is in, a type change replacing what only the old type meant, a flat node rewritten in its parts |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.StyleForm.changes/5` | `changes(kind, name, value, node, seed)`: the writes a control's submitted value means, read at its path — a font's `face` both its keys |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.StyleForm.item/4` | `item(node, path, op, seed)`: the writes an item added to (`:add`) or removed from (`{:remove, i}`) a list of nodes means — a gradient's stops — starting from `seed`'s list where the node holds none |
| `Visualize.Chart.Validator.Units.units/2` | `units(kind, key)`: the units a size of a node kind's key may be written in — none at an opacity, no `pct` where the size has no box |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.StyleForm.kind?/1` | whether a node kind is edited in its parts: a style, and a container's defaults (§3.8) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.changes/3` | `changes(kind, params, node)`: every write a change to the form means, as `{key, {:ok, value} \| :error}` pairs — a key's own, or for a style the parts the change rewrites |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.changes/4` | `changes(kind, params, node, seed)`: as `changes/3`, `seed` the style the site resolves to, which a paint's type change and a list's rows start from (§18.19) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.face/2` | the face a weight and a style show as: one of the eight words, an integer weight rounded to the nearest of the four |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.faces/0` | the eight faces in order, each with the weight and style it writes |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Fonts.keys/1` | the font keys a node kind carries |

**Signals are the chart's sources while it is designed** (#244, spec/08 §8). Each chart of the workspace carries `signals` — a map of name to `Visualize.Signals` signal — part of its structure on Save (`%{composite: %{uses, vars, signals}}`) and read back on open, since a chart without its stand-in sources draws nothing. **The data page** (#372) is what the *sources* root shows in place of its sections — the tab is titled *data* (its `aria-label`; the design key and the `phx-value-root` stay `sources`, the icon keeps its wave) — and it is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Sources.panel/1`: **one row per source of the composed design**, sorted by name, and no second list. A row is the source's **name** (a text box; `rename_source` rewrites every `marks[i].data` — an atom or a `%{source: …}` data node — every transform's `nodes` and every `default` naming it across the chart's inline layers, and moves its signal; a name another source holds is refused by notice, and a source a library layer binds is renamed nowhere and the notice says which layer); its **columns**, one line each — the column's name (a text box, `rename_column`, writing `fields` and `types` and the signal's fields together), its **type** as a select of §2.3's four with a blank for *none* (`set_column_type`), pre-filled from the generator (every column `:number` — a signal's time is its tick, an integer, spec/08 §8.2) or the host's rows (`Visualize.Data.Table.column_types/1`) when the design is silent, and an **×** (`remove_column`) — with a box under them that adds one (`add_column`, appended to `fields`); and **while designing**, a select of *none · sine · square · random · discrete* that is the source's **generator** (`set_generator`): *none* removes the signal, a kind makes one under the source's name over the source's columns (its numbers kept when it had a signal) and shows its `window`, `period`, `min`, `max` and `seed` in the row (`edit_signal`, the numbers only — a signal's fields are always its source's `fields`, written from the row, never edited apart). A source the host's pool has rows for and no signal shows *N rows from the host* in the generator's place; a source with neither says *no rows — pick a generator*. With `source_defaults` on, the row ends with the source's `default` — a box with the pool's names to pick from (a `<datalist>`), a name the pool lacks typed (`set_default_source`). The rows' columns the source does not declare are offered under the columns as picks (`+ name`, `add_column`), as the fields list once ticked them (#253); a variable among the fields (§19.9) shows as its chip, and the add box is the chip's drop target (`data-builder-bind="fields"`). The page's **+** adds a source (`add_source`: `source_n` with `fields: [:t, :value]`, `types: %{t: :number, value: :number}` and a sine generator — what *add a signal* did, under one name) in the selected layer and opens the page; a row's **×** removes the source and its signal (`remove_source`; what read it is then the undeclared-source fault, where it stands). Every write goes through the ownership rule of §18.15. **play** and **pause** (`play`, `pause`) with the tick's period stay at the page's foot. While playing the tick advances (`tick`), the pool is regenerated and the graph redraws. The pool the preview binds, and the pool list of a source site (above), is the host's `sources` **under** the chart's signals at the tick — `Visualize.Signals.pool/2` merged over the host's, so where a name is both the signal wins while designing; picking a signal fills a source's fields as picking a pool name does. A deployed design binds to the host's pool alone.

**The layer's context** is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.panel/1`, shown under the form in the *inspect* tab for the selected site whenever no style entry is chosen: the site's name; one checkbox per path its contribution carries — every top-level key, and the keys of each node under one, which are the paths `Use.mask/2` takes (§19.5) — checked where the mask holds it; and the variables the site still needs, pointing at the Variables tab. The contribution is what the site would say before its mask, `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.unmasked/2`, so a key the mask has removed is still offered to un-mask. A site masked as a whole is disabled (§19.3) and is said so rather than drawn as every box checked. The `mask` event carries the checkbox that changed; an unchecked box submits nothing, which is the path leaving.

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.panel/1` | the selected layer's context: its key control, its not-placed fault, and its free variables — the mask checkboxes are the layer row's (§18.8) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.rows/2` | `rows(fragment, use)`: one row per maskable path of a contribution — its name, whether the use masks it, its class and title — for the row's › Mask item |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.paths/1` | the paths a contribution can mask: top-level keys and each node's keys, sorted |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.toggle/3` | a use with a path added to or removed from its mask, as its checkbox says; a site masked as a whole is left alone |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Glyphs.drawn?/1` | whether a key's enum is chosen by its picture (§18.6) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Glyphs.icon/1` | the inline SVG of a control's icon — `:eye`, `:eye_off`, `:lock` for the layer row (§18.8); `:color` and `:variables` for the node row's last two buttons; a root's name for the node row (§18.13), any other root as its first letter in a ring |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Glyphs.glyph/2` | the inline SVG for one value of a drawn key, or `nil` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.keyed?/1` | whether a contribution is of a kind the document keys by name, so the panel offers the key control |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Page.styled?/1` | whether a node kind carries a style stack — a mark, a label, an axis, the legend — so its section opens with the select bar (§18.18, #378) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Page.page/1` | a root tab's node as a page of sections (§18.15): facet sections with the node keys' sub-sections inside, the frame's page as a frame, every form aimed at its node; a styled node's section opens with its select bar and holds the `style_for` slot when selected (§18.18, #378) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Page.sections/1` | the sections of a node's page — per facet the value keys and the child nodes; a frame's kind, size, binding and parts |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.form_body/1` | one node's form alone: the fields of one facet, or of the listed keys, carrying the node's label (`_node`, `data-builder-node`) and an id — `<id>-form`, the `id` given or the one derived from the node (spec/10 §15.3, #444) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Sources.panel/1` | the data page (#372): one row per source of the composed design — name, columns with types, generator or the host's rows — with +, ×, play and pause, the tick's period |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Sources.rows/5` | `rows(design, signals, pool, pool_types, owners)`: the page's rows, sorted by name — each source's columns with their types (a variable among the fields as its chip), the rows' undeclared columns to pick, its signal's kind and numbers, the host's row count, its `default`, and the declaring layer's label |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Sources.edit/3` | `edit(signal, key, value)`: a signal edited by one control of the tab — kind, fields, or a number — left as it is when the value does not read |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.structure/3` | as `structure/2`, with the chart's signals under `signals` when it has any |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.tick/2` | `tick(id, socket)`: the host's forwarding of the tick message as an update to the builder (§18.2) |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Masks.pooled?/1` | whether a contribution is a source, whose key control lists the host's pool rather than taking a name |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Stack.unmasked/2` | what a site contributes before its mask and bindings |

**A source is chosen from the pool** (#235, #238). The key control of a *source* site is the **pool list**: `<div class="vis-builder-pool" role="radiogroup">`, one `<label class="vis-builder-pool-name">` per name of the pool — the sample sources the host hands the builder (`sources`), sorted — each holding a radio (`name="key"`) and the name, laid out as `ls` lays out a directory: the names flow **down each column, then across** (CSS `columns`, the column width fitted to the names, so the count of columns follows the width the list has), the list bounded in height and **scrolling on its own** when the pool does not fit, the chosen entry marked (`vis-builder-pool-name-on`) and scrolled into view by the hook; a *none* entry first removes the key. **Beneath the list a box takes a name the pool lacks** (`key.other`) — a design may name the source a *future* host supplies, and the site's current name is kept marked in the list while the pool lacks it, so nothing is lost. A source is bound by its name, so the list spares a person guessing at what exists, and the box says what is expected. Choosing a name whose source the pool holds also **fills the source's `fields`** when it declares none — the pool's first row's keys, sorted — so a source is one pick and previews at once; fields already declared are left alone. Every other name-keyed kind — a style, a scale, a gradient, a var — keeps the box alone, since those are named by the person. In the form, a source's `default` (§2.3) is the same list under `name="default"` with its own box (`default.other`), read by `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.change/2` as the key like `key.range` is. A host with an empty pool sees the box alone, there being nothing to list.

**The typed controls in place.** A field's control is chosen from its type (§18.6), and three types now carry more than a box:

- **a color** (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor.widget/1` gives `:color`) is a well in the form, above: the color itself, selected to open the chooser, dropped on to be written. The chooser's controls are the three faces — the color table's names as `key.slot`, a hue or the picker as `key` — and a name chosen writes `color(:name)`, a hue or the picker the string, each the base of a chain the field holds (#683).
- **a reference** offers what the document declares locally and what the library holds by id, in one select — for styles, scales and sources alike: an id is spelled as the DOM carries one — `style:11` — and read back through `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Store.decode_id/1`, so `Editor.parse/2` of `{:ref, :style}` gives `{:style, 11}` for the id and an atom for anything else, and no client string of the wrong kind becomes an id. The field is also a **drop target**: the editor's form carries `Visualize.Hooks.Builder` in scope `ref`, each reference field names its key in `data-builder-ref`, and a library entry dropped on it raises `insert` with the field — the same write the select makes, refused by name when the entry is of another kind (*a scale field takes a scale; Styles::thick is a style*); a style dropped on a style field goes onto the site's stack (§18.20).
- **an enum** is the allowed values, as it was.

### 19.11 What §18 said that this section replaces

| §18 | Now |
|---|---|
| §18.2 — the toolbar's library dropdown; the save message carries `stack/1` of the layers | the library is a region (§19.8); the message carries structure (§19.6) |
| §18.3 — `list/0`, `get/1`, `put/2` over names | §19.7 |
| §18.8 — a layer is its position; four things per row | a layer is a `Use` with its own id (§19.3); select, disable-as-mask, drag |
| §18.11 — the parameters form in the tray | the variable panel in the context, chips dropped on fields (§19.9, §19.10); `Params.panel/1` stays a component a host may render on its own |
| §18.14 — a style palette that creates named styles in the selected layer | a named style is declared by a style site keyed by its name, in the design and never in a layer (§18.14, #655, D-178); a declaration in place is values; a library entry's name is the library's (§19.1, §19.2) |
| §18.16 — four regions, a store of names | three regions (§19.9); a store of ids (§19.7) |
| §18.20 — a dropped style fragment merges its `styles` declaration into the design | a dropped fragment is a `Use` with its id as `ref`; nothing is merged, and nothing is copied |
| §18.12 — an imported document is the builder's single layer | it is decomposed into sites, one kind each (§18.12) |
| §19.8 — a multi-root fragment is of kind `design` | it is no kind of fragment; the thing that is many kinds is a composite, and design is only what Deploy makes |

The rule underneath all of it: **the thing a person renames is never the thing a reference uses.** A name is an attribute of a library entry; a reference is an id; a declaration in place has neither. Nothing in the model ever has to hunt for a word.

## 20. The theme mode

**Implemented** (#534, D-139). A theme is where a host sets its look (spec/08 §7), and a theme stored as a value (§9.3) needs a tool that shows what it does. The builder has one: the **theme mode**, which is the shape the editor takes when the open chart is a theme — §19.9's rule that the editor's shape is the open fragment's kind, given a face for this kind.

### 20.1 Entering the mode

The mode is on while the selected chart is a theme — its `open` kind is `:theme` — and off otherwise; nothing else switches it. A chart becomes a theme in three ways:

- **opening a theme entry** of the library (§19.8), which opens it as a chart as every entry opens (§19.9), so *Save to library* writes the entry back;
- ***New → theme*** (`new_fragment`, §19.8), an empty theme fragment, which reads as the default theme until something is set;
- ***Theme*** on the chart bar of a composite chart (`open_theme`), which opens **the chart's own theme**: the stored entry when the design names a theme the store holds (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Store.theme_entry/2`), so saving updates what the chart draws in; else a new theme holding the built-in theme's node, or the inline theme's merged over the default, unsaved until named.

The mode writes the open site's `theme` — the selected site's local, or the first site of the chart that carries one — as every form writes a node (§18.7); a site that refers to a library entry is read-only, and an edit there is refused with the locked notice (§18.8). A theme that is a **name** in the site (`%{theme: :dark}`) becomes that theme's full node at its first edit, so the edit changes one value of the theme the person was looking at, not of the default.

### 20.2 The theme editor

In the mode the inspector (`context` `:inspect`) is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.panel/1` in place of the node's form: one `<form class="vis-builder-theme">` (`phx-change="theme_edit"`) of sections, each a `<fieldset class="vis-builder-theme-section">` with its word as the legend:

| Section | Controls | Writes |
|---|---|---|
| *theme* | the theme's `name` (a text box); *derive from*, a select of the built-in themes and the store's theme entries with a button (`theme_derive`) | `name`; derive replaces every value of the node with the chosen theme's and keeps the `name`, so a variant starts from what it varies — dark from light, color-safe from either — with the meaning held |
| *series* | one color input per series color (`series.<i>`), each with an **×** (`theme_series_remove`), then **+** (`theme_series_add`), which appends the next default series color | `series`, as the whole list; the last color cannot be removed (spec/08 §7.2) |
| *chrome* | a color input each for `background`, `surface`, `text`, `axis`, `grid` | the slot |
| *fonts* | `font_family` (a text box), `font_size`, `label_size`, `title_size` (number boxes) | the field |
| *validity* | the theme's faults — per-kind validity (§19.8), the contract faults among them — one line each in the validator's words, or *a valid theme — Save to library keeps it* | — |

A color control is `<input type="color">` showing the slot's literal as `#rrggbb` (a color `Visualize.Color.parse/1` reads but that is not hex is shown as its hex), and its title is the value as stored. A blank or unreadable value leaves the node unchanged. Saving is *Save to library* under the theme kind's per-kind validity, which refuses a theme that breaks a contract (§19.9) — the editor shows why before a save is tried.

### 20.3 The preview sheet

In the mode the main column, where a non-composite kind showed a sentence (§19.9), is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.ThemeSheet.sheet/1`: a grid of `<figure class="vis-builder-theme-sample">` cards, each a small chart drawn through `Visualize.Chart.apply/2` and `render/2` with the theme being edited and its title as the caption. The samples are fixed — `Visualize.Chart.Builder.ThemeSheet.samples/0` — because a theme is judged against everything it must serve, not against one chart:

| Sample | What it shows |
|---|---|
| *series* | eight lines colored by the categorical series, over axes |
| *status* | five bars colored `:good`, `:warning`, `:bad`, `:info`, `:neutral` — the roles (spec/08 §7.5) |
| *thermal* | a grid of cells on a `:sequential` scale ranging over the `thermal` ramp |
| *diverging* | bars below and above zero on a `:diverging` scale over the `diverging` ramp |
| *axes, grid and text* | a titled line chart with both axes, a grid and an axis label |

and a *chrome* card of swatches — every series color, then background, surface, text, axis and grid — so the colors are seen as values as well as in use. Each sample paints its frame's `background` and is rendered with `resolve: :literal`, so the sheet shows the edited theme and no stylesheet of the host's page can re-point a slot under it. The theme is built unchecked (`Visualize.Theme.build/2`), so a theme that breaks a contract still draws while it is being fixed; a ramp that cannot be drawn at all — fewer than two anchors, an anchor that is not a color — draws as the default theme's while the editor names the fault.

**Designs from the store** (`theme_designs`): beneath the samples a select of the store's composite entries and **+** (`theme_design_add`) adds one to the sheet, flattened through the store (§19.6) and applied over the host's pool and variables in the theme being edited; each carries an **×** (`theme_design_remove`). A design that does not apply shows its faults in its card, as the preview does (§18.4). The chosen designs are the builder's while a person works, not the theme's, and are not saved.

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.panel/1` | the theme editor of §20.2 |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.editable/2` | `editable(theme, store)`: the theme a site holds as the node the editor writes — a name as the whole node of the theme it names (built-in, else the store's), a map as it is, anything else an empty node |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.effective/2` | `effective(theme, store)`: the `%Visualize.Theme{}` a design's `theme` value draws as — a built-in name, a stored theme by name, or a map over the default — built unchecked, so a broken contract still draws |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.change/3` | `change(node, field, value)`: the node with one control's value written — `name`, `series.<i>`, a chrome slot, a font field — or `:error` for a value that does not read |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.series_added/1` | the node with one more series color, the default palette's at the next position, cycling |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.series_removed/2` | the node without the series color at an index; the last color stays |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.derived/2` | `derived(node, theme)`: the node with every value of the theme and the node's own `name` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.ThemeSheet.sheet/1` | the preview sheet of §20.3 |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.ThemeSheet.samples/0` | the fixed samples: each `%{id, title, design, sources}` |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.ThemeSheet.drawable/1` | a theme whose undrawable ramps are the default's, so a sheet always draws |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Store.theme_entry/2` | `theme_entry(store, name)`: the stored theme entry `theme/2` reads, `{:ok, entry}` or `:error` |

### 20.4 Ramps and roles, checked as they are edited

**Implemented** (#563, D-139). The editor's *ramps* section shows each of the four ramps of spec/08 §7.5 as one `<div class="vis-builder-theme-ramp">`, `data-ramp` its name:

- **the ramp drawn**: the color at each of 24 evenly spaced positions, `Visualize.Theme.ramp/3` of the theme being edited, so the Lab interpolation between anchors is what a person sees; on `status`, the three status roles are marked at their positions, 0, 0.5 and 1;
- **the lightness strip** beneath it: each of those colors' CIE L* drawn as the gray of that lightness (`Visualize.Color.lab(l, 0, 0)`), the anchors' L* written under it, because lightness is what a reader with no hue at all reads and what the monotonic-lightness contract protects;
- **the anchors**: a color input each (`ramps.<name>.<i>`) with an **×** (`theme_anchor_remove`, offered while the ramp has more than two), and **+** (`theme_anchor_add`), which **resamples** the ramp at one more evenly spaced anchor — each new anchor the color the ramp had at its position — so adding an anchor changes the ramp as little as the even spacing allows, and the new one is there to be moved;
- **its violation**: where the ramp breaks its contract, the section carries `vis-builder-theme-ramp-broken` and a `<p class="vis-builder-theme-violation">` with the contract's own words (spec/08 §7.5), so the fault is shown on the ramp it concerns.

The *roles* section lists the five roles. `:good`, `:warning` and `:bad` stand **at fixed positions** of `status` — 0, 0.5 and 1 — which the library fixes (D-135) and the editor shows but does not move: a role is set by the color at its position. Each status role's color input (`roles.good`, `roles.warning`, `roles.bad`) writes the status anchor that stands at its position — the first, the middle, the last; `status` with an even number of anchors has none at 0.5, so `:warning` is shown as the interpolated color with the note *add an anchor to set it* and its input disabled. `:info` and `:neutral` are colors of their own (`roles.info`, `roles.neutral`), written to the theme node's `colors` (spec/08 §7.1, #663). A role that breaks its contract carries its violation beneath it.

**Every edit is checked.** The checks are `Visualize.Theme.violations/1` — the contract of spec/08 §7.5 run on each ramp and role alone, so every broken one is named and not only the first — run on every render of the mode, which is every edit. The *validity* section lists the theme's faults by per-kind validity (§20.2); a theme with a violation is not valid, so *Save to library* refuses it with the fault (§19.9) and nothing saves a broken theme as a valid one. The theme still draws on the sheet, unchecked, while it is being fixed (§20.3).

### 20.5 Color-vision simulation

**Implemented** (#563, D-139). The editor's *vision* control (`<select name="vision">`, `theme_vision`) is one of *normal*, *deuteranopia*, *protanopia* and *tritanopia*, kept as the builder's `vision` while a person works, not stored in the theme. Under a deficiency:

- each ramp is drawn a second time as the deficiency sees it, `Visualize.Color.Vision.simulate/2` of each of its 24 colors, labeled with the deficiency, between the ramp and its lightness strip;
- the preview sheet is drawn as the deficiency sees it: every sample's and every added design's markup through `Visualize.Color.Vision.recolor/2`, and the chrome card's swatches through `simulate/2`. The sheet is rendered with literal colors (§20.3), so every color in it is simulated, an interpolated ramp color as it is drawn rather than an interpolation of simulated anchors.

The simulation is Machado, Oliveira and Fernandes (2009) at severity 1.0 (spec/08 §1.8): a model of the commonest dichromacies for judging by eye, not a measurement of any reader.

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.ThemeRamps.section/1` | the *ramps* and *roles* sections of the editor (§20.4): each ramp drawn, under the chosen deficiency (§20.5) and as its lightness, its anchors and its violation; each role at its position |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.anchor_added/2` | `anchor_added(node, ramp)`: the node with the ramp resampled at one more evenly spaced anchor |
| `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Themes.anchor_removed/3` | `anchor_removed(node, ramp, i)`: the node without the ramp's anchor at `i`; two anchors stay |

## 21. Accessibility

**Implemented in part** (#546, D-142). A chart is accessible in four ways, one work item each: a **text alternative** generated from the design and its data (§21.1–§21.4, #557), **keyboard navigation** into its points (§21.5, #558), **reduced motion** (§21.6, #559), and **status beyond hue** (§21.7, #560).

### 21.1 The summary

`Visualize.Chart.Summary.new/1` reads an applied chart (§7.3) into a `%Visualize.Chart.Summary{name, frames}`: what a screen reader says for the chart, generated rather than written, so every chart has one and it follows the data. `text/1` writes it as sentences.

**The name** is the design's `meta.name` (§2.2) as one string, else the first frame's kind with its first letter capitalized (`"Line chart"`): every chart is named.

**The frames** are summarized in drawing order (`z`, ties by name, §4.2), each as `%{name, kind, series, rows, unit, axes, extremes}` over its **data marks** — every mark but a `:rule`, `:band`, `:x_band`, `:text` or `:tiles`, which annotate data rather than draw it.

**The kind** is the first rule that holds:

| Rule | Kind |
|---|---|
| a `:geo` frame | `map` |
| a data mark is a `:needle` | `gauge` |
| a data mark's pipeline runs a layout | the layout's: `tree diagram`, `dendrogram` (`:cluster`), `circle packing`, `treemap`, `sunburst` (`:partition` under an `:arc`; `icicle` otherwise), `chord diagram`, `Sankey diagram`, `network graph` (`:force`), `contour plot`, `density contour plot`, `hexbin chart`, `Voronoi diagram`, `triangulation` (`:delaunay`), `spectrum`, `histogram` (`:bin`) |
| the first data mark, in a `:polar` frame | `radar chart` for a `:line` or `:area`, `radial bar chart` for a `:rect` |
| the first data mark | `:line` a `line chart`; `:area` an `area chart`, a `stacked area chart` under a `:stack`, a `streamgraph` under a `:wiggle` or `:silhouette` stack; `:rect` a **bar** — one edge on a band scale and the other family starting at a baseline (no field) or stacked — as a `bar chart`, a `grouped bar chart` with an `offset`, a `stacked bar chart` under a `:stack`, else a `heatmap` with a `value` channel and a `rectangle chart` without; `:symbol` and `:circle` a `scatter plot`, a `bubble chart` with a `value` channel; `:arc` a `pie chart`, a `donut chart` with an inner radius; `:rose` a `rose chart`; `:percentile_band` a `percentile band chart` |
| otherwise | `chart` |

**The value a mark draws** is what its extremes are of, and makes it a series:

- a mark under a `:stack` draws its rows' own `value` column (§5.4), not the stacked edges, named `value` and written as its `y` scale's axis writes it;
- a mark under a hierarchy layout (`:tree`, `:cluster`, `:pack`, `:partition`, `:treemap`) with a `field` draws that field, named by it;
- any other mark draws its **value channel** when that reads a field (a constant says nothing) through a scale the frame declares — a position a layout computed in pixels is not a value — or is the share of an `:arc` or a `:rose`, named by the field:

| Type | Value channel | Where a row is |
|---|---|---|
| `:line`, `:area`, `:symbol`, `:circle` | `y` | `x` |
| `:percentile_band` | `median` | `x` |
| `:needle` | `x` | — |
| `:rose` | `value` | `angle` |
| `:arc` | `value` | its name |
| `:rect`, a bar on `x0` | `y1` | `x0` |
| `:rect`, a bar on `y0` | `x1` | `y0` |
| `:rect`, otherwise | `value` | `x0` and `y0` |

A row's **name** is its `series` reading, else the first field its inline label (§5.5) reads. Where a row is, written as its scale's axis writes it after the field's name (`x 41`), is followed by its series (`in series_b`) when the mark has one — a stacked mark's `key`.

**The series** are, in order of first appearance, the `series` readings of every data mark that has the channel, the `key`s of a stacked mark, and the value's name of every other mark that draws at least one number; one name is said once. **The rows** are the first such mark's rows, else the largest mark's, and are called by its kind: `shapes` in a `:geo` frame and for `:path`, `slices` for `:arc`, `readings` for `:needle`, `bars` for a bar and `cells` for another `:rect`, `points` otherwise.

**An axis** (§4.4) says its title and its scale's domain: the range from its least to its greatest value for a numeric or time scale, its categories for a band or ordinal one — all of them up to ten, else how many with the first and the last. A scale with an empty domain says nothing. **The title** is the text of the label the design anchors at the axis (`{:axis, s}`, §6.1), else the measure's label (`Visualize.Quantity.label/1`, D-138), else the fields the frame's marks bind to the scale — the marks that draw a value when any of them does, each by its first channel of `x`, `y`, `angle`, `r`, `x0`, `y0`, `x1`, `y1`, `value`, `median` that reads a field through the scale, a stacked mark's stacked edges as `value` — else the scale's name.

**The extremes** are one per value the frame draws: the data marks that draw the same value name through the same scale — a ridgeline's layers, a scrolling series' bands — are one value, and say one highest and one lowest, each with where it is. A value of one row says that row alone (`Value 42%.`).

**A value is written as its axis writes it**, to four significant digits rather than at the ticks' step, so an extreme is not rounded to a tick: on a time scale, the axis's own tick format; on any other scale with an axis, a number by the axis's explicit `format` (a `Visualize.Format` specifier, or `:duration`), else scaled into its measure's display unit and followed by its symbol (spec/05 §4.3), else to four significant digits (`Visualize.Format` `",.4~r"`), inside the axis's `prefix` and `unit`; a value on a scale with no axis, and anything not a number, as plain text.

**The sentences** of a frame are the kind with its series — `Line chart, 1 series (y) of 51 points.`, `Line chart, 4 series: a, b, c and d.` (ten at most, then `and n more`), `Map, 127 shapes.` with none — then one per axis, `Bottom axis x, from 0 to 50.` or `Left axis day, 7 categories: Mon, …`, its side's name or, in a polar frame, `Angle axis` and `Radius axis`, then one per extreme, `Highest y 101 at x 41; lowest y 15.96 at x 50.` A design of several frames says each frame's name first (`History: Area chart, …`).

### 21.2 The root says it

`Visualize.Chart.generate/2` and `render/2` with `root: true` (§4.7) describe the chart with its summary. **The title** is `title:`, else `meta.name`, else the summary's name, so no root is unnamed. **The description** is `description:`, else `meta.description`, followed by the summary's text (a full stop between them when the description has none). `summary:` is `true` by default; a string is the caller's own text in its place, and `false` leaves the description the design's alone.

**The name and the description are wired apart** (spec/02 §2.1, amending D-56): `aria-labelledby` names the `<title>` and `aria-describedby` the `<desc>`, so a screen reader says a short name and then the summary, rather than a paragraph as the name. A root with a description and no title is labeled by the description, the only text it has. The components of spec/10 §1.2 wire their `<title>` and `<desc>` the same way.

### 21.3 The data table

`Visualize.Chart.Summary.table/2` is the data-table fallback: the rows each data mark draws, one `<table>` per mark — a mark whose table repeats another's (a gauge's fill and needle over one reading) is left out — inside a `<div class="vis-data-table">` that is **visually hidden and still read** (the clip pattern of a screen-reader-only class). A `role="img"` root presents its children to assistive technology as one image, so the table cannot live inside the `<svg>`: a host places it beside the chart.

Each table has a `<caption>` — the summary's name, then `: <source>` when there are several tables, then `(the first n of m rows)` when it is cut short — a header row of `<th scope="col">` cells named by field, and a `<tr>` of `<td>` cells per row. **The columns** of a stacked mark are its series (`key`), the field its position reads and its `value`; of a hierarchy, the fields its inline label reads and the field it is sized by; of any other mark, the fields its label reads, then its channels' fields in the order `series`, `x`, `x0`, `x1`, `y`, `y0`, `y1`, `angle`, `r`, `start`, `end`, `inner`, `outer`, `value`, `median` — a channel only when it maps through a scale the frame declares, or is a `series` or the share of an `:arc` or `:rose` — one column per field. A mark with no column has no table. Every value is written as §21.1 writes it, an absent one as an empty cell, and all text is escaped.

Options: `hidden:` (default `true`; `false` omits the hiding style), `id:` on the `<div>`, `class:` (default `"vis-data-table"`), `caption:` (default the summary's name), `limit:` the rows a table holds (default `1000`).

### 21.4 A canvas, and a hybrid chart's layers

A canvas is an image to assistive technology with nothing in it to read, so the element that stands for the chart carries the summary:

- `Visualize.Backend.Canvas.wrap_root/2` takes `title:` and `description:` ([09-rendering-backends](09-rendering-backends.md) §3.6): with a title the `<canvas>` is `role="img"` with the title as `aria-label`, and the description is a `<p id="<canvas_id>-summary">` inside it — the canvas's fallback content, never drawn — that its `aria-describedby` names.
- `Visualize.Backend.Hybrid.render/1` takes the same two and a `canvas_id:` for the `html` container's `<canvas>` (spec/09 §6.2), and its SVG layer — `render_static/1`'s root — is `aria-hidden="true"`: one layer of a picture the canvas names. The compiled chart's static, dynamic and backdrop documents (§12.4) are rendered through it and are `aria-hidden` too.
- `Visualize.Chart.Summary.attrs/2` and `description/2` name any other element that stands for the chart: `attrs(summary, id)` is `[role: "img", "aria-label": name, "aria-describedby": id <> "-summary"]`, a keyword list to spread into a HEEx tag, and `description(summary, id)` is the `<p id="<id>-summary" hidden>` it points at, the summary's text escaped. A page that stacks a compiled chart's layers (§12.4) puts them on the wrapper of the layers, the one element that is the whole picture; the gallery's chart page does, shows the summary under the chart, and follows the chart with the hidden table.

### 21.5 Keyboard navigation

**Implemented** (#558). A chart's points are reachable from the keyboard: `Visualize.Hooks.Keyboard.attrs/1` writes, once on the server, every point the summary counts (§21.1) with its position and a readout, and `KeyboardHook` makes the chart **one tab stop** in which the arrow keys move between points and series and a polite live region reads the point ([10-liveview-integration](10-liveview-integration.md) §17). Like the crosshair's arrays (D-76), the attribute serves a chart drawn as SVG and one on a canvas alike.

**The points** are, per series in the order §21.1 names them, the rows of the data marks that draw a value — the rows of one `series` reading or stacked `key`, or a mark's rows when it has neither, named by the value — in data order; marks whose series share a name are one series. A series of more than 1,000 points is **sampled** to 1,000 evenly spaced ones, its first and last kept: a keyboard does not step through 50,000 rows, and the readout still counts the series' own. Each point's **readout** is `<series>, <i> of <n>: <where>, <value name> <value>` — `y, 3 of 51: x 2, y 54.3`, `value, 1 of 6: Chrome, value 64.5` — every value written as §21.1 writes it, and its **position** the center of what it draws in the chart's own pixels: its `x` reading (the middle of `x0` and `x1` for a rectangle) and its `y` reading (`y1` for a rectangle), through the frame's scales, moved by the frame's margin and box (§4.2). A point in a `:polar` or `:geo` frame, or one whose position channels do not place it, has no position: it is read and not marked.

**A mark with an identity is reached too** (#765, D-198). A data mark that draws no number — a grid of states, a status grid, a row of glyphs — reached nothing before, so a band × band grid had no arrows and no Enter. Now such a mark's rows that carry an **identity** — the slot's declared one (spec/08 §10.1, D-167), else the inference a pick uses (spec/15 §13.2) — are a series of their own, named by the mark's `id`, else the slot it draws, else its type, every row in data order and none sampled. Each point's position is the **center** of what it draws: the middle of `x0` and `x1` and of `y0` and `y1` for a rectangle, its `x` and `y` otherwise. Its readout is `<series>, <i> of <n>: <identity>, <tooltip>`: the identity as `field value`, a composite's fields in name order (`col 4, row 4`), then the mark's tooltip template with the row's fields in it, as `TooltipHook` writes it, when the mark has one — `cells, 41 of 81: col 4, row 4, hidden`. A mark that draws numbers is read by its numbers, as before.

**A band grid is reached in two dimensions.** A series whose mark places both its axes on `:band` scales in a cartesian frame — `x` or `x0`, and `y` or `y0` — is a **grid**: each point carries its cell, its place among the distinct x positions of the series left to right and among the distinct y positions top to bottom, and `KeyboardHook` moves through it by row and by column ([10-liveview-integration](10-liveview-integration.md) §17.3). **A point of a mark that declares an event carries its key** — the event, the frame, the mark's position and the row's ordinal, what a click on the element sends (§5.7, D-170) — and Enter on it is that click.

**A board adds the order across its cells one level up** (#539). A chart is one stop and keeps its arrows; a board makes each cell one stop in its own order — Tab between cells — or, if it navigates its cells with arrows, moves focus *into* a chart with Enter and takes it back on Escape: the hook moves focus to the nearest enclosing element carrying `data-vis-cell` when Escape is pressed inside it. Neither level needs the other's details.

### 21.6 Reduced motion

**Implemented** (#559). The motion a chart has is eased: a scale's `transition` (§4.3) and a mark's (§5.1) move what they draw toward its target over `duration` milliseconds (§12.5), and a component's `animate` lets the browser tween each DOM patch ([10-liveview-integration](10-liveview-integration.md) §1.2). A reader who asks the system for less motion — `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` — gets **steps instead**: everything is drawn at its target at once, and nothing else changes.

- **On the server**, `Visualize.Chart.compile/2` takes `motion:`, `:full` (default) or `:reduce`, and holds it in the compiled chart; `Visualize.Chart.Compiled.tick/3` and `step/3` take `motion:` too, which overrides it for that tick. Under `:reduce` every scale transition and every mark motion is eased with a `duration` of `0`: the shown domain and the shown readings are the targets on the tick they change. The states are kept as under `:full` — the target, the clock, a pending shrink and its `shrink_after` hysteresis, which is a wait and not a motion — so turning `motion:` back to `:full` mid-run eases on from where the chart stands. A viewport's scroll (§12.5) is not easing: the window follows the data's time, and it scrolls under either.
- **In the browser**, `ResizeHook` adds the media query's answer to every push, `{width, height, reduced_motion}`, and pushes again with its last size when the answer changes ([10-liveview-integration](10-liveview-integration.md) §10.2): the page that sizes a chart from the hook is the page that compiles it, and passes `motion: :reduce` while the reader asks for it. A component's `animate` transition stops under the media query (spec/10 §1.2). No hook animates on its own: every motion a chart has is one of these two, or a style's motion part (§3.10).
- **A style's motion** (§3.10, §3.11, #653, D-176) is stopped in the browser, where it runs: the frame's `<style>` ends with `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` rules that make every transition instant and stop every animation and entrance, `!important` so no design beats them, `MotionHook` plays no exit, and the canvas hooks read the media query at each draw and move nothing while it matches. It needs no `motion:` from the server.

The gallery's chart page compiles with `motion: :reduce` while its `ResizeHook` says the reader prefers it.

### 21.7 Status beyond hue

**Implemented** (#560). A role (`:good`, `:warning`, `:bad`, `:info`, `:neutral`, D-135) is a meaning, and its color is one way of saying it: a grayscale print, a projector, and a reader who cannot tell red from green need another. **Each role carries a cue** besides its hue — a **glyph**, a symbol type of [04-shapes-and-curves](04-shapes-and-curves.md) §9.2, and a **dash**, a stroke pattern — `Visualize.Theme.cue/1` ([08-utilities](08-utilities.md) §7.5):

| Role | Glyph | Dash |
|---|---|---|
| `:good` | `:circle` | solid |
| `:warning` | `:triangle` | `"6 3"` |
| `:bad` | `:cross` | `"2 3"` |
| `:info` | `:square` | `"8 3 2 3"` |
| `:neutral` | `:diamond` | `"1 4"` |

**The library fixes the cues, as it fixes the roles' hue order.** A theme restyles how a role looks; it does not change which glyph means `bad`, so a reader learns the convention once, on every chart.

**A mark draws them through the role its datum has.** A channel binding `{:field, f}` (§3.2) on the marker's `shape` of a `:symbol` mark, or on the stroke's `dash` — projected as `symbol` and `stroke_dasharray` (§3.2) — reads `f` from the datum and passes it through the frame's `color` scale when the frame declares one, as a color key's binding is passed: a reading the scale maps to a role is drawn with that role's glyph or dash; any other reading is the key's own value — a symbol type, a dash — and on `symbol` a reading that is neither draws a `:circle`. So a design that colors its zones by `fill: {:field, :zone}` through `ordinal_scale(:color, domain: ["ok", "warm", "hot"], range: [:good, :warning, :bad])` marks each zone with a `:symbol` mark of `marker: %{shape: {:field, :zone}}` — the gallery's Gauge does — or outlines it with `stroke: %{dash: {:field, :zone}}`, and the zone reads in grayscale without a legend. A per-datum `symbol` is the generator's, per element (§5.6); a solid dash is the key left out. **In a `:polar` frame a symbol keeps its glyph** at its bent point (`Visualize.Chart.Mark.Bend.place/1`), as a circle keeps its radius: the glyph is drawn in pixels about its point, and bending it as `(angle, r)` — as every symbol was before #560 — drew a sliver.

### 21.8 Functions

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Visualize.Chart.Summary.new/1` | `new(applied)`: the `%Visualize.Chart.Summary{}` of an applied chart (§21.1). |
| `Visualize.Chart.Summary.text/1` | `text(summary_or_applied)`: the summary as sentences, frame by frame (§21.1). |
| `Visualize.Chart.Summary.table/1` | As `table/2` with `[]` options. |
| `Visualize.Chart.Summary.table/2` | `table(applied, opts)`: the data-table fallback as an HTML string (§21.3). |
| `Visualize.Chart.Summary.attrs/2` | `attrs(summary, id)`: the attributes that make an element stand for the chart (§21.4). |
| `Visualize.Chart.Summary.description/2` | `description(summary, id)`: the hidden `<p>` `attrs/2` points `aria-describedby` at (§21.4). |

## 22. The board mode

**Implemented** (#544, #579, D-152). A board ([15-boards](15-boards.md)) is a dashboard as data: cells on a 12-column grid, each a reference to a composite of the store, sharing a window, variables and a sync group. The builder edits one in its **board mode**, the shape the editor takes when the open chart is a board, as the theme mode is its shape for a theme (§20, §19.9). The mode is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.BoardMode`, one entry of `Builder.Modes` (§18.1).

### 22.1 A board is a library entry

**A board is a kind of fragment, `:board`**, stored as the singular wrapper `%{board: map}` whose body is the board's map form ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §2.1): every key, `version` included. So a board is versioned by its own `version`, as a theme document is (D-139), and read back by `Visualize.Board.from_map/2`, which gates the version before anything else and applies the limits ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §2.3). `Visualize.Chart.Fragment.kind/1` of the wrapper is `:board`; `Visualize.Chart.Fragment.kinds/0` holds it, so the store issues `{:board, n}` ids and `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Store.decode_id/1` reads `"board:3"`.

- **Per-kind validity** (§19.8) of a board is its reader: `Visualize.Chart.Validator.validate(%{board: map}, :board)` is every fault of `Visualize.Board.from_map/2`, each under `[:board | path]`, so *Save to library* refuses a board that does not read, with the board's own words. A board's faults **given the store** — a dangling component, an undeclared cell variable, a cell that cannot join the group ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §3.2) — are shown in the mode (§22.5) and do not block a save: a board names entries the person may be about to create.
- **The JSON form.** `Visualize.Chart.Fragment.to_json/1` writes a board fragment as `{"$version": v, "board": {…}}`, the board's body in its own JSON form ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §2.2) — a component as `{"$id": …}`, a window's time as `{"$time": …}` — with its `"$version"` stamp inside the `board` object. `Visualize.Chart.Fragment.from_json/2` reads it back through the board's codec, gating that inner stamp as it gates the outer one, so `from_json(to_json(f))` is `f` for a board as for every other fragment, and a host that keeps its library as JSON keeps its boards the same way.
- **A bundle of a board entry carries its components.** The cells' refs are ids inside the fragment, so `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Bundle.export/3` of a board entry takes their closure (§19.7) and a store's `import/2` re-keys them through its table: the imported board names the imported components. `Bundle.export_board/3` and `import_board/3` ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §11) are unchanged: they carry a board that lives outside the store.
- **A board is shown, never composed.** It is not a part of a chart: dropping a board entry into a stack is refused by name, as a wrong kind is (§18.17), and a board is never a use site's `ref`.
- **The library shows it.** `Board` is one of the kinds always shown in the kind view (§19.8), and *New* offers `board`, which opens an empty board — no cells, the defaults of [15-boards](15-boards.md) §1.2 — in the mode.

### 22.2 Entering the mode

The mode is on while the selected chart is a board — its `open` kind is `:board` — and off otherwise. A chart becomes a board by **opening a board entry** of the library, which reads its body through `Visualize.Board.from_map/2` and opens the board, so *Save to library* writes the entry back; an entry that does not read is not opened, and the notice names its faults. Or by ***New → board***. A board chart holds the `%Visualize.Board{}` being edited, not a stack of uses: it has no layers to show, so its row in the *Charts* panel offers no **+**, and *Save*, *Theme* and *Deploy*, which are a composite's, are not on its chart bar.

**Every edit keeps the board valid alone.** Each write of the mode builds the board it would make and validates it alone ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §3.1); a board with a fault is not written, and the notice names the fault in the board's words (`Visualize.Board.format_error/1`). So the board the mode holds always reads, and what *Save to library* stores always opens again.

### 22.3 The grid: placing and resizing cells

In the mode the main column is the **grid** (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.BoardGrid.grid/1`): `<div class="vis-builder-board-grid">`, a CSS grid of 12 equal columns, rows the board's `row_height` high and `gap` apart (`Visualize.Board.layout/1`), the arithmetic of [15-boards](15-boards.md) §1.6. Each cell is an `<article class="vis-builder-board-cell">` placed with `grid-column` and `grid-row` at its `x`, `y`, `w` and `h`, carrying `data-board-cell` (its name) and `data-board-x`, `-y`, `-w` and `-h`, `tabindex="0"` and an `aria-label` of its title and its rectangle. It shows its title (else its name), its component's name, its preview (§22.6), and three **handles**, `<span data-board-handle>` of `e`, `s` and `se`, on its right edge, its bottom edge and its corner. A click selects the cell (`board_select`).

**The hook moves; the server places.** `BuilderHook` in scope `board` ([10-liveview-integration](10-liveview-integration.md) §16.3) drags a cell by its body and resizes it by a handle, showing the rectangle it would take as the pointer moves, and pushes `board_place` with `{cell, x, y, w, h}` when it is released. The server is the authority: `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Boards.place/3` writes the rectangle and validates the board, and refuses — leaving the cell where it was, with the notice — a cell that runs off the grid (`{:off_grid, 12}`), one that overlaps another (`{:overlap, other}`), or a rectangle whose keys are not integers of the right sign. So whatever a client sends, the board holds no overlap and nothing off the grid. With a cell focused, the arrow keys move it by one square and Shift with an arrow resizes it by one, through the same push.

**Adding and removing.** *Add a cell* (`board_add`, a select of the store's composites) places a new cell, 4 columns by 2 rows, at the **first free place in reading order** (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Boards.free/3`): the first row, then the first column, where a 4-by-2 rectangle overlaps nothing; it is named `cell_<n>`, the least `n` no cell has, since a cell's name is its identity and the builder makes no atom of a person's text. Its component is the chosen composite. With no composite in the store there is nothing to add, and the form says so. *Remove* (`board_remove`) removes the selected cell.

**The reflow is shown.** *Wide* and *narrow* (`board_view`) switch the grid between the placement and the board **below its breakpoint**: the cells as `Visualize.Board.arrange/2` stacks them at a width of `breakpoint − 1`, in reading order, each across all 12 columns, in a column as narrow as a phone's, with the sentence *below <breakpoint> px the cells stack in reading order*. The narrow view is a view: its cells carry no handles and are not dragged, and the placement is edited in the wide one.

### 22.4 The cell and the board

In the mode the inspector (`context` `:inspect`) is `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Boards.panel/1` in place of the node's form: one `<form class="vis-builder-board">` per part, each a `<fieldset class="vis-builder-board-section">` with its word as the legend:

| Section | Controls | Writes |
|---|---|---|
| *cell* — the selected one | its title; its component, a select of the store's composites; *joins the sync group*, a checkbox; one text box per variable its component declares; *Edit component*; *Remove* | the cell's `title`, `ref`, `sync` and `vars` |
| *add a cell* | a select of the store's composites and *Add* | a new cell (§22.3) |
| *board* | the name and the description | `meta` |
| *window* | *none*, *last* or *fixed*; the seconds of *last*; the UTC *from* and *to* of *fixed*, each a `datetime-local` read as UTC; the display *zone* | `window` and `zone` |
| *sharing* | the sync group (empty is none); *stale after*, seconds (empty is never) | `sync` and `stale_after` |
| *layout* | breakpoint, row height and gap, pixels | `layout` |
| *variables* | one text box per variable any cell's component declares | the board's `vars` |
| *validity* | the board's faults given the store (§22.5) | — |

**A variable's name is one a component declares.** A cell's variables are the variables its component's design declares (spec/14 §2.4), read from the flattened component; the board's are those of every cell's component. A name from the page is matched against those names and never made an atom. A value is read as a number when it is one, else kept as the text; an empty box removes the value, so the cascade ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §5) falls through to the board's or the component's default. A board variable that no component declares — one stored before its component changed — is listed with its value, to be removed.

**The window.** Choosing *fixed* where the window was not fixed starts it at the last whole hour before now; *last* starts at 3600 seconds. The zone is checked as the board checks it ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §1.5): a zone the host's database does not know is refused.

### 22.5 The board's faults

The *validity* section lists `Visualize.Board.validate/3` of the board given the store — a cell whose component the store no longer holds, a component that is not a design, a cell variable the component does not declare, a cell that cannot join the board's group — one line each in `Visualize.Board.format_error/1`'s words, or *a valid board — Save to library keeps it*. A cell with a fault carries `vis-builder-board-cell-faulty`, so the grid shows where. **The faults are read once per change of what they are a function of** (#723, D-191): the board and what the store says of each component its cells name — the component flattened through the store, nested references and all (§19.8) — so a pass that changes neither reads the faults the last one made, and a pass over a store whose entries changed under the same store term reads them again. Each cell's slots (§22.8) are read the same way, per cell: the cell and what the store says of its component.

### 22.6 The preview

**Each cell is realized alone**, through `Visualize.Board.realize/4` of a board holding that one cell, its placement and the board's shared state — so the cell's component is resolved through the store, its variables are the cascade, the board's group and zone are written into it, and it is applied at its box's size at a container width of 1200 pixels, exactly as the runtime's cell process applies it ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §6, §8.3). One cell at a time, so a cell that cannot be applied shows its own faults and every other cell still draws.
